O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show interface trunks

Trunk Line Native Tag         Prune Set

------------------------------------------

e0-0 down 0001 No             allow 1-4096

e0-2 down 0022 No             allow 1-4096

e1-3 down 0050 No             deny 30-70

which, among other things, displays for each defined trunk:

· Native: the defined native-vlan for this trunk

· Tag: the tagging state of the above mentioned native-vlan

· Prune Set: the set of allowed or denied VLANs on this trunk

7.4 Creating VLANs

The most straightforward way to create a new VLAN is to explicitly name its VID using
vlan command. It is possible, if needed, to associate a literal description using the
same command:

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure vlan 10 name test_vlan

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show vlans

VLAN Name                              AccessPorts             TrunkPorts

==============================================================================

---- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -

0001 default_vlan                      e0-0 e0-1 e0-2 e0-3

                                       e1-0 e1-1 e1-2 e1-3

                                       e1-4 e1-5 e1-6 e1-7

                                       e2-0

---- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -

0010 test-vlan

In this way you have just created a new VLAN with VID 10 named test_vlan; note that
since the only things which matters in VLAN management in the end is VID number,
you can safely omit to name the VLAN: in such a case the system will automatically
generate a VLAN name based on its VID in the form V0010.

7.5 Managing VLANs ports membership

After having defined a new VLAN with the desired VID as showed above, you still
have to explicitly associate the desired ports to the VLAN itself using the interface
configuration level command switchport; the following will set up a couple ports in

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                                      59/253
VLANs

access and trunk mode:

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA[configure]>interface e1-4 switchport mode access vlan 10

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA[configure]>interface e2-0 switchport mode trunk native vlan 10

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA[configure]>interf e1-5 switchp mode trunk prune allow 10,15-25

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA[configure]>do show vlans

VLAN Name                    AccessPorts          TrunkPorts

==============================================================================

---- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -

0001 default_vlan            e0-0 e0-1 e0-2 e0-3  e2-0

                             e1-0 e1-1 e1-2 e1-3

                             e1-5 e1-6 e1-7

---- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -

0010 test-vlan               e1-4                 e1-5 e2-0

Please note that the starting tokens of the fourth command has been shortened to fit
the line width of this manual; but they are correctly accepted by the menu since there
is no prefix ambiguity with any other valid menu token.

As you can see by the above show vlans command output, we have just associated to
VLAN 10, the port e1-4 as an access-port and the port e2-0 as a trunk native for VLAN
10: this means that from now on each and every VLAN (not pruned) which will be
created will be forwarded on trunk e2-0, taking care to transmit every egressing frame
tagged except for the frames belonging to the user-defined trunk native VLAN 10,
which are instead transmitted untagged.

Moreover, we defined also interface e1-5 as trunk-port, and we associated a pruning
interval so that the only vlans allowed to be forwarded on trunk e1-5 will be VLAN 10
and the VLANs in the range 15-25 inclusive.

The prune set for a trunk can be defined as:

· the set of allowed VLANs:

         O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>conf interf e1-5 switchp mode trunk prune allow 10,15-25

· the set of denied VLANs:

                O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>conf interf e1-5 switchp mode trunk prune deny 10,15-25

System default is allow 1-4094, which means that no pruning is enforced on every
newly created trunk, unless explicitly requested by configuration.

     IMPORTANT NOTE

     Setting non homogeneous pruning sets among the defined trunks can lead
     to inter-switch connectivity troubles on the pruned set of VLANs, because
     the standard RSTP process currently implemented on the switch simply
     ignores the VLANs existence (and their pruned state) when it has to determine
     the active topology, and eliminating possibly physical loops.

Using switchport command you are free to move ports directly between already
defined or still non-existent VLANs, both in access and in trunk mode. Using the no-
form of switchport command, you can remove the port from a VLAN and put it back
to the default native VLAN 1. If you do not specify the VLAN membership while using
command switchport, the default VLAN with VID 1 is assumed, thus the following

60/253                                        Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                            VLANs

commands will have both the same effect:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure interface e1-4 switchport mode access vlan 10
      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e1-4 switchport mode access

An alternative way to create a new VLAN in the system is to simply define a port mode
using switchport command with a non-existent VID; in this way the new VLAN will
be created implicitly; this works for both access or trunk port modes.

Take a look at the following command sequence issued in the above scenario:

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e1-7 switchport mode access vlan 22
VLAN 22 did NOT exist. Created vlan 'V0022'.

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-3 switchport mode trunk native vlan 66
Required trunk-native VLAN 66 did NOT exist. Created vlan 'V0066'.

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show vlans

VLAN Name                                 AccessPorts          TrunkPorts

==============================================================================

0001 default_vlan                         e0-0 e0-1 e0-2 e1-0  e0-3 e2-0
                                          e1-1 e1-2 e1-3 e1-5
                                          e1-6

0010 test-vlan                            e1-4                 e0-3 e1-5 e2-0

0022 V0022                                e1-7                 e0-3 e1-5 e2-0

0066 V0066                                                     e0-3 e2-0

      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

We have indirectly created two new VLANs, simply setting a port in access or trunk
mode and naming a still not existent VID; note that in such a case you cannot specify a
name for the VLAN so the default name is generated for you by the system upon
creation. However, you can change such name using an explicit vlan command:

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure vlan 66 name demoring

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show vlans

VLAN Name                                 AccessPorts          TrunksPorts

==============================================================================

...

0066 demoring                                                  e0-3 e2-0

...

------------------------------------------------------------------------------

When you define a new VLAN, traffic tagged as belonging to that VLAN will be
forwarded on each switch port configured in trunk mode, for which the new VLAN is
not pruned, and on each switch port configured in access mode for that VLAN. Thus,
defining a VLAN without explicitly assigning access ports makes sense, since all
incoming traffic tagged as belonging to a VLAN which has not been defined on the
switch would be otherwise discarded.

7.6 Destroying VLANs

Although you can remove all ports from a VLAN by putting them back to default
VLAN 1 using the switchport command, an already existent VLAN is not destroyed
simply because it has no more access or native trunks associated with it. Thus, it

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                                       61/253
VLANs

remains forwarded through all existent trunks, for which it's not pruned, until explicitly
removed from the system.

Let us suppose this scenario:

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show vlans

VLAN Name                     AccessPorts                    TrunkPorts

==============================================================================

---- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -

0001 default_vlan             e0-0 e0-1 e0-2 e1-0            e0-3 e2-0

                              e1-1 e1-2 e1-3 e1-5

---- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -

0010 test-vlan                e1-4 e1-6                      e0-3 e1-5 e2-0

---- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -

0022 V0022                    e1-7                           e0-3 e1-5 e2-0

You can explicitly remove a whole VLAN using the no-form of vlan command. In this
case if the VLAN was not empty yet, the system could tackle this condition in two
different ways:

· when no trunk has been defined as native for this VLAN, it proceeds to remove
    the whole VLAN, by removing in advance all the access ports defined for it,
    and then removing the VLAN itself from the list of existent VLAN forwarded
    by any existent trunk on system:

         O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure vlan 10
         Deleting Vlan 10.
         Removing also all its access ports: e1-4 e1-6

· if a trunk has been previously defined as native for this VLAN, the system
    refuses to take further action: in such a case you first manually remove the
    native trunk using explicitly the switchport command:

                O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure vlan 22
                Cannot delete Vlan 22.
                Trunk e2-0 marked as native for this vlan.

In this case you can remove such VLAN in the following way:

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure interface e2-0 switchport mode trunk
O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure vlan 22
Deleting Vlan 22.
Removing also all its access ports: e1-7

62/253                              Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                               Quality of Service (QoS)

Chapter 8 - Quality of Service (QoS)

8.1 QoS on Ethernet packets

Quality of Service allows you to prioritize packets so that time sensitive data can be
transferred smoothly and with minimal delay over your network.

Priorities are assigned to packets as they enter the switch by inspecting their QoS
fields.

Packet QoS requirements can be defined at MAC level (layer 2 of the OSI reference
model) or inside the layer 3 protocol header.

Note that layer 2 QoS fields are lost as soon as the packet is forwarded by a layer 3
device (router), while layer 3 QoS fields are maintained.

8.2 Layer 2 QoS

The Consist Switch supports layer 2 QoS as defined by the IEEE 802.1p specification.
This is an extension of the IEEE 802.1Q (VLAN tagging) standard and works in tandem
with it.

802.1Q standard specifies a tag added to the Ethernet header in order to carry VLAN
information. This tag has two parts: the VLAN ID (12-bits) and a Class of Service (CoS)
value (3-bits).

The CoS bits provide space for 8 different levels of service as listed below:

CoS          Traffic characteristics
0 (lowest)   Best effort
1            Background
2            Spare
3            Excellent effort
4            Controlled load
5            "Video" < 100 ms latency
6            "Voice" < 10 ms latency
7 (highest)  Network control

                                      Table 5: Class of Service (CoS) levels
Because of the use of 802.1Q tagging, layer 2 QoS should be used on trunk ports only.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  63/253
Quality of Service (QoS)

Normal access ports, in fact, discard tagged frames.

On access ports you can associate incoming packets with a user defined CoS value
which will be used inside the 802.1Q tag if the packet is transmitted tagged.

8.3 Layer 3 QoS

The Consist Switch supports layer 3 QoS for IP packets as defined in RFC 2474/2475
Differentiated Services Architecture (Diff Serv).

Diff Serv uses the 6-bit Differentiated Services Code Point (DSCP) field in the standard
IP header for packet classification purposes. This field replaces the 3-bit IP precedence
field originally used to classify and prioritize types of traffic.

QoS treatment is defined as Per- Hop Behavior (PHB) and is determined by DSCP
values.

Up to 64 different DSCP values can be defined. However, most networks use only the
following PHBs:

    · Default PHB: associated with DSCP 0, typically best effort characteristics.

    · Expedited Forwarding (EF) PHB: characteristics of low delay, low packet
         loss and low jitter, suitable for voice, video and other real-time traffic.

    · Assured forwarding (AF) PHB: it defines 4 different levels of forwarding
         assurances for IP packets AF classes. Each class has 3 different levels of drop
         precedence. The combination of classes and drop precedence yields 12
         separate DSCP encoding from AF11 through AF43. Should congestion occur
         between classes, the traffic in the higher class is given priority.

    · Class selector (CS) PHB: it maps the TOS precedence bits into a Diff Serv
         class. So there are 8 DSCP encoding from CS0 to CS7.

8.4 Priority levels

Priorities are assigned to packets as they enter the switch by inspecting the layer 2 or
layer 3 QoS fields. The priority value is then used when the packet has to be
transmitted from the output port. Each port has 8 independent output queues, one for
each supported priority. Packets are transmitted in the order received for any given
priority. Output queues are numbered from 1 to 8, with queue 8 having the highest
priority and queue 1 the lowest.

The order the frames are transmitted is controlled by the scheduling mechanism
associated to the port. Two different schedulers are available:

    · weighted: an 33, 25, 17, 12, 6, 3, 2, 1 weighing scheme is applied to the 8
         priorities. This prevents low priority traffic from being starved by high priority
         traffic.

    · strict priority: all top priority frames are transmitted until that priority queue

64/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                               Quality of Service (QoS)

         is empty. Then the next lower priority queue is served. Using this approach, the
         packets with the lowest priority are transmitted only when there are no other
         higher priority packets to send. In some cases a high rate of packets with high
         priority can prevent packets with lower priority from being sent.

     NOTE

     On Consist Switch equipped with firmware version 6.x the number of queues
     is limited to 4 (this is shown clearly by the CLI menu help messages) and the
     weighing scheme is 8, 4, 2, 1.

By default, the weighed mechanism is used, but you can change this setting and define
how many queues should be served using the strict priority mechanism, choosing a
number of queues from 1 up to 6 or all queues.
For example, from interface-specific configuration level, the command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 qos prio-queues 1

specifies that queue 1 should be served using the strict priority mechanism while the
other queues will continue to use the weighed mechanism.

8.5 Priority assignment to incoming packets

As mentioned before, the priority level assigned can be based on the 802.1p CoS field
contained inside 802.1Q tagged frames or on the DiffServ Code Point (DSCP) field
contained in the IP packets header.
You can choose how priority is assigned on a per interface basis. For example the
command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 qos trust cos

defines that the initial priority should be assigned basing on the 802.1p CoS field
value, while the command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 qos trust dscp

forces the use of the IP DSCP code point value as the priority selector.
By default, ports are configured to trust both fields with precedence given to 802.1p
CoS field (qos trust both).
Note that IP Diff Serv is a layer 3 mechanism for carrying QoS information. It is
preserved while packets flow across a switched network and can't be changed by layer
2 switches.
802.1p, instead, is a layer 2 mechanism, it is contained only in 802.1Q tagged packets
and can be respected or forced by layer 2 switches.
When an untagged frame enters a port configured to trust the CoS field, it will be
assigned the port default CoS value. If the frame is already tagged, the CoS value will
remain unchanged.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  65/253
Quality of Service (QoS)

You can override the CoS assignment of incoming packets forcing the default CoS
value of the port to be assigned to all packets. The command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 qos default cos 2 override

sets the default CoS value for the port e0-1 to 2, forcing this value on all packets that
enter the port.

Remember that the 802.1p CoS value is maintained only if the packet is forwarded
tagged out from a trunk port.

8.6 Priority mapping

A priority map is used to associate CoS and DSCP values to one of the eight available
priority levels, that is the 8 egress queues.

You can inspect the current mapping using the command:

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show qos prio-map

Queue CoS                 DSCP

----- --------------- ----------

10                        cs0

21                        cs1,af11,af12,af13

32                        cs2,af21,af22,af23

43                        cs3,af31,af32,af33

54                        cs4,af41,af42,af43

65                        cs5,ef

76                        cs6

87                        cs7

----- --------------- ----------

On Consist Switch equipped with firmware version 6.x the mapping is slightly different
since the number of available priority queues is limited to 4:

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show qos prio-map

Queue CoS                 DSCP

----- --------------- ----------

1 1,2                     cs0,cs1,af11,af12,af13

2 0,3                     cs2,af21,af22,af23,cs3,af31,af32,af33

3 4,5                     cs4,af41,af42,af43,cs5,ef

4 6,7                     cs6,cs7

----- --------------- ----------

If required you can change this mapping for the CoS and DSCP values respectively.
For example the command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure qos cos-map 1 0,1,2,3

assigns a priority level of 1 to the first four 802.1p CoS values.
You can also remap the DSCP code points. The command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure qos dscp-map 2 af41,af42

maps the code points Assure Forwarding 41 and Assure Forwarding 42 to the priority
queue 2.

While on Consist Switch equipped with firmware version 6.x the DSCP code points can
be referred to exclusively using their string aliases, from the firmware version 7.2.1 the
DSCP code points can be referred to either using their string aliases or using their hex

66/253                                        Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                 Quality of Service (QoS)

values. The following table reports the available aliases and their associated values:

Code point alias  Hex  Bitmask         AF32  28  011100
                       000000          AF33
CS0               00   001000          CS4   30  011110
                       001010          AF41
CS1               01   001100          AF42  32  100000
                       001110          AF43
AF11              10   010000          CS5   34  100010
                       010010          EF
AF12              12   010100          CS6   36  100100
                       010110          CS7
AF13              14   011000                38  100110
                       011010
CS2               16                         40  101000

AF21              18                         46  101110

AF22              20                         48  110000

AF23              22                         56  111000

C3                24

AF31              26

8.7 Rate limiting

Under some circumstances it may be required to limit the network traffic entering or
leaving a switch port in order to guarantee the planned overall level of QoS.

For example, if you have associated a port to the highest priority level and you are
using a strict prio-queue for serving that priority level, you might want to be sure that,
if the end device connected to the port begin transmitting packets in uncontrolled
way due to, for example, some failure on the end device itself, this will not have any
effect on the whole network.

In this case, if you know that the end device should not produce more that 2 Mbps of
data, you could limit the maximum rate accepted by the port to that value.

Another requirement could be connected with the level of broadcast traffic. Too many
packets of this type can be problematic and it can result in potentially severe network
congestion.

All these issues can be easily resolved by using the rate limit feature.

The interface command

      switchport rate-limit inbound|outbound|broadcast <value> [K|M|G]

can be used to define the maximum value of traffic rate accepted or emitted by the
selected port. The rate can be expressed in kbps, Mbps (by default) and Gbps2.

For example, the following command limit to 5 Mpbs the traffic entering port e0-2:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-2 switchport rate-limit inbound 5 M

As the incoming traffic exceeds this limit, the switch begins to drop packets entering
port e0-2.

The following command limit to 1 Mbps the amount of broadcast traffic accepted by
port e1-4 reducing the impact of what is commonly called a broadcast storm:

2 In the firmware series version 6.x the Gbps unit is not available.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                    67/253
Quality of Service (QoS)

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-2 switchport rate-limit broadcast 1 M

Outbound traffic values can be also specified in PPS (Packets Per Second):

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interf e0-2 switchport rate-limit outbound 10000 PPS

These commands can be used together on the same interface.
Broadcast limit is a subset of the overall incoming traffic and can be associated with a
lower value.

     NOTES

     - Rate limit settings with inbound direction and broadcast direction are mutually
     incompatible.
     - The rate limiting is meaningful for physical interfaces only; related commands
     cannot be issued on link aggregations (group interfaces).

8.7.1 Priority queues rate limiting

Rate limiting settings can also be applied to the priority queues defined in the Priority
mapping section.
The interface command can be used to define the maximum value of queued traffic
rate accepted by the selected port:

      [no] configure interface NAME switchport rate-limit inbound VALUE UNIT prio QUEUE

where QUEUE represent one of the eight priority queues (a value in the range [1..8]3).
For example, the following command limits to 5 Mpbs the traffic with priority mapped
to queue 1 entering port e0-2:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-2
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-if e0-2]>switchport rate-limit inbound 5 M prio 1

     NOTE

     A priority queue rate limit setting with inbound direction is incompatible with a
     not priority based rate limit setting with inbound direction.

8.7.2 Profiles

Rate limit settings can be group together in a profile and applied to multiple
interfaces.
The following command will create a profile (or remove it in the no-form):

      [no] configure rate-limit profile NAME

3 On Consist Switch equipped with firmware version 6.x the number of queues is limited to 4
    and this is shown by the CLI menu help messages.

68/253                    Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                             Quality of Service (QoS)

Multiple settings can be defined in a profile as follows:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure rate-limit profile mylimits inbound 1 M prio 4
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure rate-limit profile mylimits inbound 2 M prio 3
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure rate-limit profile mylimits outbound 7 M

In the example the profile has been given the name mylimits.
A profile of rate limit settings is applied to a switch port interface by the command:

      [no] configure interface INTERFACE switchport rate-limit profile NAME

As an example, the mylimits profile is applied to interface e1-0:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e1-0
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-if e1-0]>switchport rate-limit profile mylimits

Any change to a profile will apply to all the ports the profile has been applied to.

8.7.3 Committed Burst Size

The Consist Switch implements the rate limiting feature using the leaky bucket
scheme. The behavior of the bucket for the inbound traffic can be finely tuned up by
specifying the minimum size of a data burst that the bucket should be able to
accommodate.

The actually applied value is shown together with the other details of any physical
interface. The following is the output for an interface with no limits applied:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show interface e1-1 details
      ...

          Rate Limiting
          ------
          outbound unlimited
          inbound unlimited (burst n/a)

The following is the output for an interface limiting both the outbound and broadcast
traffic:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show interface e2-0 details
      ...

          Rate Limiting
          ------
          outbound kbps:34000
          inbound kbps:4032 (burst bytes:135299)

This feature and its details described in the next sub-sections is not available on
Consist Switch equipped with firmware series version 6.x.

8.7.3.1  User supplied Committed Burst Size

A Committed Burst Size value, in Bytes, can be specified by the user as optional
parameter to the commands described above, per interface:

      configure interface NAME
                 switchport rate-limit inbound|broadcast VALUE K|M|G burst BURST [prio QUEUE]

or per profile:

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                                                69/253
Quality of Service (QoS)

      configure rate-limit profile NAME
                                                        inbound|broadcast VALUE K|M|G burst BURST [prio QUEUE]

The supplied value (BURST) overrides the default one that sometimes can result in
slightly inaccurate rates (especially for TCP traffic).

8.7.3.2 Automatic optimal Committed Burst Size
An optimal Committed Burst Size value can be automatically computed by the device.
This facility is enabled by the use of the auto token instead of a numeric value, as
follows:

      configure interface NAME
                   switchport rate-limit inbound|broadcast VALUE K|M|G burst auto [prio QUEUE]

or per profile:

      configure rate-limit profile NAME
                                                          inbound|broadcast VALUE K|M|G burst auto [prio QUEUE]

     IMPORTANT NOTE

     The automatic computation of the optimal burst size can last up to some
     seconds. The use of this feature can significantly increase the boot time that
     can last up to some minutes (in the worst case). A suggested good practice is
     to use this feature once, then read the computed value and use it in the final
     deployed configuration in place of the auto token.

70/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                                       Time

Chapter 9 - Time

System date and time can be shown with the command show system time. The
output will be similar to the following:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show system time
      Fri 13 May 2011 14:52:40 [UTC]

System clock is not backed up by an internal battery so the system time and date must
be re-assigned on every boot or synchronized with external sources by means of one
of the supported time protocols.
Components used and system design ensure that time keeping is the most accurate
possible, but if you want to keep many devices synchronized with greater accuracy you
should rely on a time synchronization service. The system can be configured to use one
of such services (see Auto-synchronization section below).
To obtain information about the current clock configuration, you can use the
show clock command. The output will be similar to the following:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show clock
      Current time: Fri 13 May 2011 14:51:27
      Time protocol: none
      Time server(s):
      Timezone: Universal Coordinated Time

9.1 Configuration

In order to set the current time you need to be logged in as admin. First you need to
set the timezone with the command

      configure system timezone ZONE COUNTRY

For example:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system timezone Europe Rome

You can obtain a list of valid values for ZONE and COUNTRY by pressing `?` while typing
the command. This setting will be saved as soon as a save running-config command
is issued.
In order to manually set the time issue the command

      sysadmin set time DD MM YYYY HH mm SS

The meaning of the parameters is the following:

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  71/253
Time

    DD        day of the month
    MM        month
    YYYY      year
    HH        hours
    mm        minutes
    SS        seconds
For example:

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>sysadmin set time 02 08 2017 10 08 15

9.2 Auto-synchronization

The system clock can be configured for automatic synchronization with one or more
time sources, by means of TIME or NTP protocols.
The protocol to be used for automatic synchronization can be set with the command:

      configure system clock source PROTOCOL ADDRESS [ ADDRESS ]

where valid values for PROTOCOL are ntp and timep, and ADDRESS is the IP address of
a reachable network resource. You can specify up to two server addresses.
Once the synchronization service has been configured, the device will synchronize
regularly with time sources.

9.3 NTP client

If the system clock is configured to be automatically synchronized with NTP (as NTP
protocol client), the user can obtain information about the status of the protocol
issuing the show ntp status command. The output will be similar to the following:

A@SW-Std.80-31-60>show ntp status

Reference ID : AC100164 (172.16.1.100)

Stratum       :6

Ref time (UTC) : Tue Apr 02 07:17:37 2019

System time   : 0.000000000 seconds fast of NTP time

Last offset   : -102256784.000000000 seconds

RMS offset    : 102256784.000000000 seconds

Frequency     : 0.000 ppm slow

Residual freq : -156.536 ppm

Skew          : 1000000.000 ppm

Root delay    : 0.013945554 seconds

Root dispersion : 2.441490173 seconds

Update interval : 0.0 seconds

Leap status   : Normal

By default the NTP client polls the time source (server) every 16 seconds. This interval
can be made longer or shorter using the command configure ntp client polling-
time TIME where TIME is a value (in seconds) in the range 1-8192. Once set, this
configuration becomes effective after a device reboot.

72/253                                        Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                                       Time

     NOTE
     The polling-time parameter is rounded up to an actual value that is the closest
     power of two (1, 2, 4, 8, 16, ...). For example, any value in the range 7-12
     corresponds to an actual value of 8 seconds; lower values will be mapped to 4
     (or less), greater ones will be mapped to 16 (or more).

9.4 NTP server

The Consist Switch can act as time source (NTP server) for other devices in the network.
This functionality is not enabled by default and can be toggled with the command
[no] configure ntp server enable.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  73/253
                                                                                                   Security

Chapter 10 - Security

The Consist Switch lives in a heterogeneous network environment, so it is mandatory
to adopt all the options to guarantee the right security level.

10.1 Users

The access to the system CLI is protected by an authentication phase during which a
user must supply the right credentials to gain access to the system.
In the Consist Switch there are 3 predefined users, reflecting different roles for
professionals interacting with the device.
The operations those users are allowed to perform on the switch are exactly defined by
their role. These roles are:

   user: this role is entitled only to watch at the device conditions without any
      chance to modify its behavior. This is the safest role.

   operator: this role gains the possibility to access the global or specific
      configuration level. This is the normal role used to configure the switch.

   admin: this role has total control over the device. It grants all the privileges and,
      because of this, it allows to use the system administration level commands. These
      commands deal with the most critical aspects of the device such as firmware
      upgrades, etc...

The initial password for those users are set equal to the user name itself.
The passwords may be changed by a configuration level command, however restricted
to the admin role. For the user operator, for example, the following command must be
used:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system operator password SecretPassword

This command is recorded in the configuration file with the password encrypted.
If, for any reason, passwords are lost, they may be changed back to their factory values
with a low level system operation detailed in Password Recovery, page 205.

     NOTE

     There is a group of commands that, for consistency sake, can only be issued
     by the admin role whilst defined outside the system administration level.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  75/253
Security

10.1.1 Enforcing password security

The password security strength can be enforced enabling the password complexity
check feature.
If the check is enabled then any new password has to contain at least one lowercase
and one uppercase letter, one number and one special character; furthermore it has to
be at least 8 characters long.
This feature is turned off by default and can be activated with the following command:

      configure system password complexity enable

10.1.2 Account locking

A user account can be locked for a given amount of time after a given number of failed
login attempts. Account locking is off by default and can be enabled with the
following command:

      configure system login failed-attempts <1-100> [lock-timeout <1-1000000>]

The lock timeout part is optional and has a default value of 180 seconds.

     NOTE

     Account locking policy can not be used together with RADIUS Server
     authentication and it will be ignored in case RADIUS authentication is
     configured.

10.2 CLI Users RADIUS Authentication

The previously described CLI authentication phase can also be completed leveraging
an external Remote Authentication Dial In User Service (RADIUS) authentication
server.
In order to use such RADIUS based authentication for local users
(user/operator/admin) you'll have at first to define a proper set of RADIUS parameters,
and then to associate such radius based authentication-method to each desired user;
some specific privileges' restrictions may apply when performing such configurations.

10.2.1 Configuring RADIUS Authentication

As already said, in order to use RADIUS authentication you must at first define the
parameters needed to identify and successfully communicate with the chosen
RADIUS authentication server (you must have admin full administration privileges to
define primary and backup radius-servers and theirs options):

76/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                       Security

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system radius-server primary 172.16.16.111 secret
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system radius-server backup 172.16.16.112 secret2
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system radius-server backup options auth_port=1814

where possible radius options are showed in the following help excerpt:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system radius-server primary options <optionlist>
                          configure and enable primary radius server options:
                          optionlist is opt1=val1[,opt2=val2[,opt3=val3...]]
                          where optx are: auth_port, acct_port, retry_cnt, timeout, src_ipaddr.

After having configured a valid server we can choose what kind of authentication-
method to use for each defined CLI local user:

    · Forcing local authentication only (default) for user user

                A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system authentication user local

    · Forcing radius authentication only for user user

                A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system authentication user radius

    · Allowing both RADIUS and local authentication for user user; in this last case,
         at first, user authentication is attempted using the defined external RADIUS
         server(s), and then system falls back to usual local authentication-method:

                A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system authentication user radius local

In the above examples we issued all the needed shell commands as user admin, but
keep in mind that when dealing with the choice of the authentication-method the
following privileges' restriction applies:

    · user user cannot change either his/her own authentication-method, since have
         no access to configure level commands

    · user operator can change authentication-method for himself/herself and for
         the lower-privileged user user, but cannot change authentication-method for
         higher-privileged user admin

    · user admin can change authentication-method for any user

10.3 802.1X Port Authentication

The device supports port-based authentication through standard protocol 802.1X-
2004; the Consist Switch embeds an 802.1X PAE Authentication server, that, after being
properly configured and enabled, can be used to filter clients' access to physical ports
depending on the outcome of 802.1X authentication exchanges. The device is not able
to act as a 802.1X PAE Supplicant.

10.3.1 802.1X: Overview and terminology

Some terminology and concepts used to explain 802.1X configuration and operation:
    · PAE - Port Access Entity: a logical entity associated to a physical LAN port, that

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  77/253
Security

         supports one or both of the 802.1X roles of Supplicant and/or Authenticator.

    · Supplicant: it's the name used for the external client trying to get access to the
         network via 802.1X port authentication schemes. Usually it's a software layer
         embedded into the client's operating system. The Consist Switch does not
         support this role as a PAE, so we'll use this term to refer only to an external
         client attempting access.

    · Authentication Server: it's the ultimate entity which decides if the supplicant-
         provided credentials were good, and access is to be allowed or not. In our
         scenario it's an external RADIUS Authentication/Accounting server.

    · EAP (Extensible Authentication Protocol): it's the inner protocol used to
         securely carry authentication information between supplicant and
         authentication server.

    · EAPOL (EAP Over Lan): this is the outer layer 2 protocol carrying all the
         authentication exchanges between the supplicant and the authenticator. It's
         the only type of traffic allowed on the 802.1X controlled port till authentication
         is successfully completed.

    · RADIUS: this is the outer layer 3 protocol used by the authenticator to relay all
         the inner EAP authentication exchanges, received from the supplicant via
         EAPOL frames, to the RADIUS Server.

    · Authenticator PAE: it's the intermediate system (this device) which talks
         directly with the Supplicants and Authentication Server and it's in charge of:

          effectively inhibiting access and blocking all traffic on UnAuthorized ports
              except authentication related frames' exchanges during the initial 802.1X
              authentication phase.

          relaying EAP requests from the Supplicant to the Authentication Server
              properly unpacking such requests from containing EAPOL, and repacking
              them into well formed RADIUS packets, well-known to the final
              authentication system.

          relaying EAP responses from Authentication Server to the Supplicant
              properly unpacking such responses from containing RADIUS packets, and
              repacking them into well formed EAPOL frames, well-known to the final
              Supplicant entity.

          granting access to successfully authenticated supplicants.

          maintaining/updating access-accounting information with the specified
              accounting server (where configured).

         Additionally, from the perspective of the 802.1X Authenticator PAE a port can
         be configured in the one of following states:

          force-authorized: it's the default state in which a port is brought-up when
              802.1X is not configured on that port. All traffic flows are allowed and no
              filtering is enforced on such a port; 802.1X does not care about any EAPOL
              frame possibly traveling across this port. The port is in fact not managed at
              all by 802.1X Authenticator. A port can be forced into this state at any time

78/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                       Security

              through CLI commands.

          force-unauthorized: when in this state all traffic flows are instead
              inhibited and any EAPOL frame is likewise ignored by the 8021.X
              Authenticator PAE; also in this case the port is practically not managed by
              802.1X. A port can be forced into this state at any time through CLI
              commands.

          auto: when a port is set to this state, the 802.1X Authenticator PAE starts
              managing such port and, if 802.1X is properly configured, the
              Authenticator starts also relaying any authentication exchange from the
              Supplicant to the AuthenticationServer. When a port is in this state only
              EAPOL authentication frames are allowed to flow through the port, till
              Supplicant credentials are successfully verified. A port can be configured in
              this state at any time through CLI commands.

Moreover, we should consider that a port controlled by 802.1X could be connected not
only directly to a supplicant end-device, but also to an intermediate device like a
switch/hub or an access-point; in this particular configuration we could end up having
multiple per-port end-devices trying to get access to the LAN. To address such needs
an 802.1X controlled port can be configured also to act in two different modes:

    · host-mode single: in this mode each and every supplicant hitting the port
         MUST be authenticated before being granted access to the LAN.

    · host-mode multi: in this mode instead only the first supplicant is in charge to
         perform a successful 802.1X authentication: once this first client, called
         hereinafter a sentinel, is authenticated, the port is virtually opened to any
         other client which shows up after, even if these subsequent clients won't be
         able to authenticate themselves (sort of guest nodes); but in this case, as soon
         as the sentinel fails re-authentication and disappears all the possibly
         unauthorized guest nodes are immediately forced out, while any other
         regularly authenticated supplicant (other than the sentinel) continue to hold
         the granted access.

Finally, when a proper and complete 802.1X configuration is in place, a controlled port
can transition also to one of the following states:

    · authorized_single: this port was set in host-mode single and at least a
         supplicant has already been authenticated.

    · authorized_multi: this port was set in host-mode multi and at least a
         supplicant has been authenticated (the sentinel). The access is then allowed to
         any following client on this port without the need of any additional
         authentication for those clients; this is true as long as the first authenticated
         client, the sentinel, remains authenticated.

10.3.2 802.1X Basic Configuration

Briefly, a basic and functional 802.1X Authenticator PAE configuration must fulfill at
least the following areas:

    · define an aaa-domain which holds the parameters needed to reach an active

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  79/253
Security

         and configured RADIUS Authentication Server from the Consist Switch
         hosting the 802.1X Authenticator.

    · associate such aaa-domain to the port(s) on which you want to enable 802.1X
         access control.

    · enable dot1x on such port(s)

Defining an aaa-domain for the back-end RADIUS Server simply means associating a
domain name to a group of parameters needed to effectively contact and
communicate with an external back-end RADIUS Authorization/Accounting Server(s).

Every radius aaa-domain can contain up to four server identity comprising:

    · 2 Authentication Servers: primary and backup

    · 2 Accounting Servers: primary and backup

The minimum required aaa-domain configuration must include at least one
authentication server in order to be used for 802.1X access control. An example of
basic configuration involving two distinct authentication and accounting servers
follows:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure aaa-domain
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[aaa-domain]>radius myDomainName auth-serv primary address

                                                                                                172.16.16.10 secret mySecret
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[aaa-domain]>radius myDomainName acct-serv primary address

                                                                                                172.16.16.100 secret mySecret2

The chosen domain name must be one single word not exceed 32 characters in length.

At this point we can continue carrying on our 802.1X configuration on the desired
ports.

Say you want to enable 802.1X access control on port e0-3 using the above specified
RADIUS Authentication and Accounting Server parameters, you have simply to:

    · associate the desired aaa-domain to the port:

                A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-3 dot1x aaa-domain myDomain

    · enable 802.1X PAE Authenticator functionalities on such port:

                A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-3 dot1x port-control auto

From now on such port will be managed by 802.1X using the specified external
RADIUS Server; you can see progress of an authentication exchanges enabling dot1x
debug:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>debug dot1x
      ...
      ... after a supplicant successfully authenticated on port
      ...
      dot1x |EVENT [e0-3] -- link-status up
      dot1x |[e0-3] -- Registered supplicant 000000000343 as sentinel.
      dot1x |EVENT [e0-3] -- AUTH-COMPLETE 000000000343 172.16.16.10

80/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                              Security

NOTE

When defining an aaa-domain holding the parameters for the external radius
server, keep in mind that end-to-end full connectivity should exist between the
Authenticator PAE and such remote RADIUS Authentication Server for all this
to work: this means, obviously, that must exist a valid route to get to such
remote machine, but also, less obvious, that the 802.1X traffic flow must be
routed through a switch physical port which belongs to the native Vlan 1
(one), since that's the only vlan currently associated to the switch routed-
interface vlan1.

10.3.3 802.1X Status and Debug

We can enable dot1x debug messages issuing:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>debug dot1x

An example output could be:

dot1x  |EVENT [e0-3] -- link-status up
dot1x  |[e0-3] -- Registered supplicant 000000000343 as sentinel.
dot1x  |EVENT [e0-3] -- AUTH-COMPLETE 000000000343 172.16.16.10

which shows that:

· link status changed for a dot1x controlled port

· a supplicant is marked as sentinel being the first authenticated supplicant on
    such port

· basic authentication data is briefly reported as:

dot1x |EVENT [e0-3] -- AUTH-COMPLETE 000000000343 172.16.16.10

                   ^^^  ^^^            ^^^           ^^^

                   |    |              |             |

                   {port} {type event} {mac-address} {used AuthServ IP addr}

Possible authentication events reported in debug messages are:

· AUTH-COMPLETE: a new unknown supplicant has successfully authenticated.

· AUTH-CONFIRMED: an already authenticated supplicant has successfully
    renewed its authenticated state: this could be due to re-authentication
    attempts triggered both side (by Supplicant or by Authenticator)

· AUTH-FAILED: a new or known supplicant has failed first authentication or re-
    authentication. This incident will be reported into log file too.

We can obtain data about 802.1X port states and authenticated supplicants also on
demand using the proper show dot1x informational command:

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                                         81/253
Security

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show dot1x supplicants

Ifname dot1XState               HostMode

==============================================================================

e0-1 force-unauthorized         single

e1-2 auto                       multi

e1-4 auto                       multi

e1-5 force-unauthorized         single

e1-6 auto                       multi

e2-0 authorized_single          single

          ----------------------------------------------------------------------

          Supplicant  Vlan LastAuth                              ActiveAuthServ

          ----------------------------------------------------------------------

          000000000343 * 1      Fri 1 Jan 2010 00:30:15 [UTC] 172.16.16.100

Issuing the above command you'll see that for each port, in a state other than force-
authorized, are reported:

· its dot1xState:

          · force-unauthorized

          · auto

          · authorized_single

          · authorized_multi

· its HostMode:

          · single

          · multi

· a table containing any currently authenticated supplicant, listing for each of
    these:

          · its mac-address

          · an * mark indicating that such supplicant is the chosen sentinel for
              such port

          · the assigned VLAN (for the client and for the port)

          · the last time authentication has successfully completed

          · the currently active (last used) Authentication Server IP address

10.3.4 802.1X Advanced Configuration

Apart the basic 802.1X configuration we've seen so far, it's possible to fine tune also
the following other dot1x related parameters accessible from the dot1x per-interface
menu level:

    · eapol-version: this is needed for being able to choose between version 2
         (default) and version 1 EAPOL frame format; needed to address some specific
         issues with some old supplicant's client software not properly supporting new
         version frames.

                A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-0 dot1x eapol-version 1

82/253                                    Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                                   Security

    · host-mode: this can be used to select between single (default) and multi
         authentication host-mode (as previously described).

                A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-0 dot1x host-mode multi

    · reauth-timer: this is used to specify the per-port re-authentication interval to
         use; it's the amount in seconds between Authenticator triggered re-
         authentication attemps: when set to 0 (zero) it effectively disables re-
         authentications attempts as a whole. Default is 0 (zero): disabled.

                A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-0 dot1x reauth-timer 120

Additionally, it's possible also to instantly trigger a forced re-authentication on a
chosen port; such action can be triggered on all supplicants currently authenticated
on that port or on a single specific supplicant using its MAC address:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-0 dot1x reauthenticate station all
      dot1x |EVENT [e2-0] -- AUTH-CONFIRMED 000000000343 172.16.16.10

or

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e2-0 dot1x reaut station 00:00:00:00:03:43
        dot1x |EVENT [e2-0] -- AUTH-CONFIRMED 000000000343 172.16.16.10

10.3.5 802.1X ­ Maintenance Access Via Local Users
      Back-end

Standard 802.1X port security solutions are generally cumbersome to configure and
maintain: as we have seen, we should configure a few redundant Radius server in
addition to the required switch local configurations.

Even though this is an acceptable scenario on a large scale deployment with multiple
Radius authority configured to serve a multitude of supplicant end-devices, when,
instead, the typical use case would be to simply allow access to the network for only
maintenance reasons to authorized personnel, such complex Radius back-end
configuration is particularly demanding.

In order to address these needs VDS Rail provides a special 802.1X based port-security
solution that does not depend on the configuration of an additional
authentication server, nor external neither internal.

After having enabled 802.1X support on the selected ports in the usual way, the
conceived solution permits to easily associate such ports to a special 'aaa-domain'
named local-db: using such aaa-domain as back-end, port access will be granted to
any 802.1X Supplicant that uses valid local users credentials (admin / operator / user);
such binding will remain effective no matter what kind of users authentication was
chosen (local vs radius), and across any possible change of authentication tokens
through different means (password change both via KMsh and TTCMP Network
Designer). In other words if you have access to the management functionalities of the
switch you will have access also to any 802.1X secured port configured to use the
local-db special aaa-domain.

The only limitation of such mechanism is that the 802.1X Supplicant MUST be

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  83/253
Security

configured to use EAP-TTLS/PAP as EAP authentication method: in this way a TLS
tunnel will be established during phase1, while in the following phase2 the user
credentials will travel in clear inside such encrypted channel using simple PAP. This
double mechanism requires the 802.1X Supplicant client to configure not only the
desired user/password credentials BUT also to provide a valid VDS provided CA
certificate: such certificate will be effectively used by the client to authenticate the
server (VDS switch) and to bring up the outer TLS tunnel.

In the following you can see how such a configuration is made, and how a supplicant is
successfully registered using the 'local-db':

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>debug dot1x
A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e1-7
A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-if e1-7]>dot1x port-control auto
dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- port-control auto
A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-if e1-7]>dot1x aaa-domain local-db
dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- link-status down
dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- link-status up
dot1x |[e1-7] -- Registered supplicant 1c7508e6ae29 as sentinel.
dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- AUTH-COMPLETE 1c7508e6ae29 local-db
A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-if e1-7]>do show dot1x supplicants

Ifname dot1XState            HostMode

==============================================================================

e1-7      authorized_single  single

          ----------------------------------------------------------------------

          Supplicant  Vlan LastAuth    ActiveAuthServ

          ----------------------------------------------------------------------

          1c7508e6ae29 * 1   Fri 1 Jan 2010 02:34:01 [UTC] local-db

e2-0 auto                    single

It's anyway anytime possible to revert such a port to a standard authentication back-
end server in the usual manner; in the hypothesis that an aaa-domain named
testradius has been configured in advance:

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>debug dot1x
A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-if e1-7]>dot1x aaa-domain testradius
dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- link-status down
dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- link-status up
dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- link-status up
dot1x |[e1-7] -- Registered supplicant 1c7508e6ae29 as sentinel.
dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- AUTH-COMPLETE 1c7508e6ae29 172.16.16.10
A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-if e1-7]>do show dot1x supplicants

Ifname dot1XState            HostMode

==============================================================================

e1-7      authorized_single  single

          ----------------------------------------------------------------------

          Supplicant  Vlan LastAuth    ActiveAuthServ

          ----------------------------------------------------------------------

          1c7508e6ae29 * 1   Fri 1 Jan 2010 02:38:53 [UTC] 172.16.16.10

e2-0 auto                    single

      dot1x |EVENT [e1-7] -- AUTH-COMPLETE 1c7508e6ae29 172.16.16.10

Please note that even though anytime you change the chosen aaa-domain to local-db
or viceversa, the 802.1X port state will be toggled up and down and re-authentication
itself will be triggered, the exact behavior in terms of timings depends very much
indeed, on the chosen 802.1X client Supplicant software: for this reason it is possible to

84/253                                 Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                                   Security

obtain far more predictable results when performing such a configuration change
after having unplugged the related port, in order to be sure that the client re-
authenticates in a small time as cable is plugged in again next time. This is not an issue
anyway in a standard boot sequence, where a well defined configuration is in place
way before the port itself is enabled.

A typical 802.1X Supplicant configuration using EAP-TTLS/PAP as authentication is
depicted in the following figure (from a Ubuntu Linux system with NetworkManager);
note that the user, after having selected the proper external and internal methods
(Tunneled TLS and PAP), provides not only the proper credentials (in this case the
standard default) but also a VDS certificate used for the TLS tunnel establishment.

                        Figure 10.1: Example of 802.1X Supplicant configuration

10.4 Communication services

It is possible to manage the Consist Switch in different ways.
While the serial console is always available, other services that permit such
communications may be enabled or not.
In detail, the communication services are:

    · SSH: a secure protocol, usually enabled
    · telnet: a less secure way to interact with the device, usually disabled
    · HTTP-based REST-like web services: a communication paradigm best suited

         to be used from the applications, based on the HT TP protocol.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  85/253
Security

    · HTTPS-based REST-like web services: same as the above, based on HT TPS
         instead, so much stronger on the security aspects.

    · L2 console: The only network mechanism providing a connectivity with the
         Consist Switch without an IP address, using uniquely the device MAC address.

Although some of the listed services are enabled by default at boot time, they may be
switched on or off with some specific configuration commands:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure service ssh-server enable

     NOTE

     These commands require an admin role to be executed.

10.5 Login banner

A customized banner can be displayed to the user before any interactive login. This
feature can be enabled and the message customized by means of the command:

      configure system login banner "<customized message>"

Double quotes are mandatory. New lines can be inserted by typing \\n, which will
appear in the command line as a \n.

This is an example

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure system login banner "UNAUTHORIZED ACCESS TO THIS

DEVICE IS PROHIBITED\n"

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>exit

# ssh admin@10.1.2.3

UNAUTHORIZED ACCESS TO THIS DEVICE IS PROHIBITED

Password:

** Konuendo Platform Configuration Shell **

**         Railway Switch Version  **

**         Press '?' for Help      **

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>

86/253                                       Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                       DHCP services

Chapter 11 - DHCP services

The Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP) is a protocol defined in RFC 2131 to
pass configuration information to hosts on an IP network.
This chapter describes the DHCP services available on the Consist Switch: per-port
DHCP server, DHCP snooping, DHCP option 82 and DHCP trusted interfaces.

11.1 Per-port DHCP server

The Consist Switch is equipped with an integrated DHCP server which eases the setup
of the network environment by handling the automatic assignment of an IP address to
DHCP clients connected to a specific port of the Consist Switch.
The IP addresses offered to clients are configured on a per-port basis and do not
require any specific information about the clients itself, such as, for example, its MAC
address, unless multiple clients should be connected to the same port. This way the
client device can be replaced without changing the configuration of the Consist
Switch.
The configuration parameters offered to the connected devices are distinguished in
two types:

    · port-specific parameters, valid for a given port only; they can be defined in the
         specific configuration level of the port;

    · shared parameters, valid for many ports and collected in named groups called
         DHCP options groups; they can be defined in the global configuration level.

At port level, the DHCP server is automatically enabled if at least one of its parameter is
set in the configuration.
At global level, for the DHCP requests being handled by the server, the latter must be
enabled using the following command from global configuration level:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server enable

The no-form of the command will disable the service globally, and thus also on each
port.

11.1.1 Single-client configuration

If a single end-device will be connected to the port, a unique IP address is required.
To offer a specific address to whichever client performs a request on a given port, use
the command:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server client-address ADDRESS [ NETMASK ]

The NAME parameter specifies the port that the server is being configured on. The

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  87/253
DHCP services

NETMASK parameter is optional: if not present, the natural mask of the IP class the
address belongs to is used. The no-form of the command will remove such
configuration.
In the following example we set the IP address and the network mask that will be
offered to a client connected on e0-1 port:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-address
                                                                                                             10.14.1.1 255.255.255.0

11.1.2 Multi-client configuration

For more complex requirements, where the Consist Switch should be able to support
more than one single device per port, two different types of configurations are
available: pool-based and selector-based.

11.1.2.1 Pool-based

In pool-based configurations, the addresses which will be offered to clients are defined
as a range with the following command:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server client-address-pool START END

Where START and END are initial and final IPv4 addresses of the range from which the
server will choose the one to offer. The END address must be inferior than the START
address, or the command will fail.
For example the following configuration:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1dhcp-server client-address-pool
                                                                                                                      10.1.1.1 10.1.1.10

will make the server offer clients the first available address in the range from 10.1.1.1
up to 10.1.1.10 (included).

11.1.2.2 Selector-based

For a finer control of address allocation, selector-based configurations can be defined
to make the server offer different addresses depending on specific information found
in the DHCP messages received.
There are four types of selectors available, and each one qualifies clients as follows:

    · hostname: consider option 12 (Hostname) in the DHCP message;
    · client-id: consider option 61 (Client-identifier) in the DHCP message;
    · vci: consider option 60 (Vendor Class ID) found in the DHCP message;
    · vlan: consider the 802.1q (VLAN) tag found in the packet carrying the DHCP

         message; this selector is meaningful only if the port which the server is active
         on is configured as a VLAN trunk port.
Each configuration line which includes one of the above selectors is also called a
selector-based rule.

88/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                           DHCP services

The general form of a command which defines a selector-based rule is the following:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server client-address
                                                                                          ADDRESS [ NETMASK ] SELECTOR VALUE

where SELECTOR is one of hostname, client-id, vci or vlan, and VALUE depends on
the selector type as follows:

    · hostname: a string specifying the expected value of option 12;
    · client-id: the type of the option 61 value (ascii or hex) and a string

         specifying the expected value;
    · vci: a string specifying the expected value of option 60;
    · vlan: an integer between 1 and 4094 which specifying the VLAN ID.

It is possible to define any number of selector-based rules, but it is not possible to mix
rules based on different selector types for each port.

If a DHCP discovery/request message does not match any of the defined rules, no
answer is provided.

A rule based on client-id selector describes also how the selector's value should be
considered when comparing it with the actual option's value:

    · ascii: the selector's value must be specified as an ASCII string; the type field
         (first byte) in the option's value is assumed to be 0, and the remaining bytes are
         compared to the selector's value;

    · hex: the selector's value must be specified as a sequence of bytes expressed in
         double-digit hexadecimal form; the option's value is then compared byte-by-
         byte to the selector's value, including the type field (first byte).

For more information on the meaning of option 61 value consult RFC 2132.

In the following example we configure the server to offer two different addresses to
clients which announce themselves with host-1 and host-2 values as option 12
(hostname selector):

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-address
                                                                               10.16.1.1 255.255.255.0 hostname host-1

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-address
                                                                               10.16.1.2 255.255.255.0 hostname host-2

Same as the above example, but using vlan selector:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-address
                                                                                                10.16.1.3 255.255.255.0 vlan 3

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-address
                                                                                                10.16.1.4 255.255.255.0 vlan 4

Same as the above example, but using client-id selector:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-address
                                                      10.16.1.5 255.255.255.0 client-id ascii host-1

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-address
                                                      10.16.1.6 255.255.255.0 client-id hex 0103040b01a088

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  89/253
DHCP services

11.1.2.3 Fallback address

There are cases where some of the devices logically connected to a given port and
needing to obtain their IP address via DHCP are unknown or considered so by purpose.
For known devices it is possible to define which specific address will be offered them
by means of one or more selector-based rules. For unknown devices it is however
required that they somehow obtain an address, possibly undefined a priori.
To let unknown clients obtain a valid address, the per-port DHCP server provides the
concept of fallback address.
A fallback address is an address offered when none of the defined selector-based rules
match the request.
Fallback addresses can be obtained in two, mutually exclusive ways:

    · picking the first available address from a defined fallback pool, or
    · forwarding the DHCP message on the network and letting another server, if

         any, handle it.
To define the fallback pool for a given port use the command:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server fallback address-pool START END

while:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server fallback forward-mode

makes the server to forward to the network any unknown request coming from a given
port.
With the above command, the message will be forwarded out from all the ports of the
Consist Switch, except the one it came from.

11.1.3 Configurable options and fields

The following options and fields can be set in messages sent by the server of the
Consist Switch through a specific port.

11.1.3.1 Hostname (option 12)

The configuration of option 12 (Hostname) is compatible only with single-client
configurations or with multi-client configurations using vlan selector rules. In all other
cases this option cannot be configured.
To configure option 12 use the command:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server client-name HOSTNAME [ vlan VID ]

The no-form of the command will remove such an option.
Please note that the only supported selector for this option is vlan. If the port is
configured to serve multiple clients, the address configuration must be based on the
vlan selector only.

90/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                           DHCP services

In the following example the host name ip-cam1 will be included in any DHCP offer:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-name ip-cam1

The following host names will be included in DHCP offers depending on the VLAN tag
found in received packets:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-name host-2 vlan 2
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-name host-3 vlan 3

11.1.3.2 Boot file name

It is possible to set the DHCP messages Boot file name field (file) value with the
command:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server client-boot-file
                                                                                                   FILENAME [ SELECTOR VALUE ]

where the optional SELECTOR parameter can be one of client-id or hostname
selectors. The client-id selector supports only ASCII values.
The no-form of the command will set this field to "no value".
Please note that the above command will not add the option 67 (Bootfile name) but
instead set a field in the DHCP message.

11.1.3.3 Provisioning URL (option 66)

It is common practice in the industry to use DHCP option 66 (TFTP server name) to
inform a client of a known network location from which it can fetch data for its
purposes (a common example is application-specific configuration data). This is why
option 66 in VDS Rail devices is called Provisioning URL.
To set the value of option 66 use the command:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server client-provisioning URL

The no-form of the command will remove such an option.

11.1.4 Groups of options

A number of DHCP options can be shared among different ports by defining an
options group and then associating such a group with the desired ports. The options
defined in the group will then be included in all DHCP replies transmitted out from
those ports.
Using groups it is possible to avoid configuring the same options for many ports where
the DHCP service is enabled.
A group is defined by the following command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME

The no-form of the command will remove the group definition.
For example:

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  91/253
DHCP services

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure ip dhcp-server group ip-cameras
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-dhcp-g ip_cameras]>

The above command creates a group named ip-cameras and then enters the specific
configuration level for this group. Notice that the prompt has changed accordingly
with the new configuration context.
Once you have defined the group by its name, you can specify the options it includes
as in the following examples:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-dhcp-g ip-cameras]>default-router 192.168.1.254
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-dhcp-g ip-cameras]>dns-server 192.168.1.10
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-dhcp-g ip-cameras]>netmask 255.255.255.0
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA[config-dhcp-g ip-cameras]>time-server 192.168.1.10

When a port has been associated with a group and at the same time its DHCP server
configuration includes the definition of a network mask, the latter definition takes
precedence over the one from the group, if any.
Once the configuration of the group is terminated, the exit command will cause the
shell to return to the global configuration level.
Once defined, the group can be associated with one or more ports with the following
command:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME dhcp-server client-group GROUP

The no-form of the command will remove the group association.
For example, from the specific configuration level for interface e0-1:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 dhcp-server client-group ip-cameras

11.1.4.1 Subnet Mask (option 1)

To add option 1 in a group definition use the command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME netmask ADDRESS

The no-form of the command will remove the option from the group.

11.1.4.2 Router (option 3)

To add option 3 in a group definition use the command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME default-router ADDRESS

The no-form of the command will remove the option from the group.

11.1.4.3 Time server (option 4)

To add option 4 in a group definition use the command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME time-server ADDRESS

At most one address can be specified. The no-form of the command will remove the
option from the group.

92/253         Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                           DHCP services

11.1.4.4 Domain Name Server (option 6)

To add option 6 in a group definition use the command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME dns-server ADDRESS

At most one address can be specified. The no-form of the command will remove the
option from the group.

11.1.4.5 Lease time (option 51)

By default the lease period is equal to 1 hour.
Since there is no permanent storage of lease information, the latter will be lost when
the Consist Switch reboots.
To add option 51 in a group definition use the command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME lease-time MINUTES

Where MINUTES is a value between 1 and 1440 (1 day). The no-form of the command
will remove the option from the group.

11.1.4.6 Server identifier (option 54)

As stated in RFC 2131:
         «To accommodate potentially incomplete network connectivity, a server MUST
         choose an address as a 'server identifier' that, to the best of the server's knowledge,
         is reachable from the client.»

It is possible to specify a given IP address as option value within the definition of a
group with the command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME server-id ADDRESS

The no-form of the command will remove the option from the group.
If not explicitly set, the server will use the IP address of the management interface
(vlan1), if defined, as the option's value in its messages.
Note that this is a "virtual" IP address because it does not need to be configured on any
Consist Switch interface in order to be used. The server will respond to all messages
targeted to this address.

11.1.4.7 Classless Static Route (option 121)

To add a static route, option 121, in a DHCP group definition use the command

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME static-route NETWORK MASK ADDRESS

The no-form of the command will set such field to "no value".
For each group only a single static route definition is currently supported.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  93/253
DHCP services

11.1.4.8 Next server address field

To configure the value of the next server address (siaddr) field in DHCP messages use
the command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME next-server ADDRESS

The no-form of the command will set such field to "no value".

11.1.4.9 Server host name field

To configure the value of the server host name (sname) field in DHCP messages use the
command:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-server group NAME sname ( ADDRESS | HOSTNAME )

The no-form of the command will set such field to "no value".

11.2 DHCP snooping

When enabled, DHCP snooping forces all DHCP messages entering the Consist Switch
to be processed before being forwarded.
DHCP snooping performs three major tasks:

    · it keeps a record of which IP addresses are currently allocated to hosts
         downstream of the device's ports;

    · it allows the dynamic insertion/removal of DHCP option 82 (Relay Agent
         Information) into/from DHCP packets that flow through the device;

    · it allows a fine control on the DHCP protocol handshaking by checking that
         ports that are receiving a DHCP response from a server are trusted by the
         system.

To globally enable or disable the DHCP snooping service use the following command
when in global configuration level:

      [ no ] configure ip dhcp-snooping enable

DHCP snooping is enabled by default on all Consist Switch devices.
To enable or disable the service on a specific port, use the following command:

      [ no ] configure interface NAME ip dhcp-snooping enable
