Status       published                         not listening

14.3 SNMP

The device currently supports the Simple Network Management Protocol
(SNMP) v1/v2c/v3. In the absence of any SNMP specific configuration, an SNMP v2c
Agent is enabled by default with two usual communities defined: a public read-only
community and a private read-write community.

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CC>show running-config
      ...
      ...
      snmp-server community public ro
      snmp-server community private rw
      snmp-server enable

The SNMP Agent configuration is available at the global configuration level - submenu
snmp-server, as you can see asking for the submenu on-line help:

122/253                                        Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                               Monitoring and diagnostics

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server    (? pressed)

community     Set the SNMP server community value

contact       Set the SNMP contact value

description   Set the SNMP server description

enable        Enable SNMP services

engineid      Configure manually an SNMPv3 engine-ID for the local

              agent

exit          Exit from SNMP subsystem level

group-access  Configure access capabilities for an SNMPv3 group

host          Set the SNMP server target host address for egressing

              traps

location      Set the SNMP location value

name          Set the SNMP server name

traps-all-if  Configure emission of selected traps on all available

              interfaces at one time

traps-global  Configure emission of global generic traps.

traps-ttcmp   Configure emission of TTCMP related traps

user          Configure an SNMPv3 user

view          Include the specified oid-tree value in the set of

              allowed OIDs for the named view

Beside the configuration commands, the enable command is the one which effectively
starts up the SNMP Agent if a minimum set of required parameters has been
configured. Every SNMP configuration command issued on a running (enabled)
SNMP Agent has instead immediate effect without the need of restarting the SNMP
subsystem, apart from the snmp-server engineID command which is not effective until
agent is restarted via enable command or at the next reboot.

14.3.1 Configuring SNMPv2 Community-Based
      Authentication

In the following we will describe each one of the above configuration parameters, but
first keep in mind that, in order to start the SNMP subsystem, at least one community
string or one SNMPv3 user must be defined in advance, as you can see from the
following output obtained trying to start the SNMP subsystem without any settings
already in place:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server enable
      SNMP Subsystem NOT STARTED !
      At least ONE v1/v2c community OR v3 user MUST be configured for the SNMP
      subsystem to be enabled.

The string defined by the community command is the means by which SNMP v1/v2c
authenticates a user connection demanding an SNMP query: you can define how
many communities you need in the switch but such community names must be
unique; if you simply define a community name the SNMP subsystem will declare by
default such community to be a read- only one and the access will be set unlimited to
the whole MIB OID tree and without any further limitation regarding the IP source
address of the incoming connection. As an example let us establish a couple of
communities:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server community vds-private rw
      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server community public ro

and verify the added configuration lines:

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                             123/253
Monitoring and diagnostics

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show running-config

      ....

      interface vlan1
          ip address 172.16.16.111 255.255.0.0
          enable

      snmp-server community public ro
      snmp-server community vds-private rw

In this way you have just simply defined two communities: one with read-only ro
access, and the other with full read-write rw access capabilities. If you need a finer grain
control to the access of SNMP-exposed data, you can additionally specify a specific
MIB OID which will be the root of the MIB tree accessible from that community, using
the oid sub-command of community as in the following example:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server community public rw oid .1.3.6.1.2.1.2.2

This last configuration line will redefine a new read-write community named public
with restricted access to the ifTable of the standard IF- MIB; in fact the above OID could
also have been specified using the symbolic specification format of the MIB OID tree
for ifTable:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server community public rw oid IF-MIB::ifTable

Additionally, note from the above example that, since the community name is the
unique key of the configuration line, you can simply override a previously defined
settings for the community public issuing a new and extended community command
including also the additional OID limitation while changing also the read-write access
mode for such community.

Another more flexible mean of restricting the user to access only a subset of the whole
available SNMP data is to use the view access control model; later on you will see how
you can define an SNMP view which includes a set of one or more sub-trees of the
whole SNMP MIB tree and associates it with a user-chosen name. After having defined
such a named view, you can easily bound such partition of the MIB tree to a defined
community, thus effectively further restricting access to a controlled set of MIB objects
for some class of users.

So, in the following example, if you suppose to have already created a view named
alfaCentauri, issuing then the following command:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server community public rw view alfaCentauri

you will establish a new access restrictions for the public community instead of the
previous restriction based on OID identifier. The view and OID sub-commands for
access control are mutually exclusive.

Finally you can decide to further restrict the access to a certain community not only
depending on what they're asking, but also depending on where they're asking from:
in other words you can additionally associate some source IP address restrictions with
a defined community. So extending the above example:

124/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                       Monitoring and diagnostics

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server community public rw view
                                                                                                alfaCentauri access 172.16.16.1

this additionally restricts such community to the specified IP address. This kind of
restriction works only for single IP address: you cannot specify multiple IP addresses of
whole subnets while restricting access to SNMP data.

14.3.2 Configuring Views

As already said, a view represents a named subset of one or more sub-trees of the
whole MIB tree: it is a useful tool to partition such tree and refer to a specific part of it
to enforce some particular access restrictions.

In order to reference a view as a mean of access restriction inside a community
configuration line, you must also define it, describing how it is made up and what it
refers to. Using multiple snmp-server view commands you can create a named view
and at the same time specify the exact sub-tree which you want to include or to
exclude from the view itself. Let us make a few examples on this. The following creates
a view which is composed only of the named ifTable:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>config snmp-server view alfaCentauri IF-MIB::ifTable included

Once such a view is associated with a community directive as stated below:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server community public rw view
                                                                                                alfaCentauri access 172.16.16.1

you will be sure that, from now on, the public community will have read- write access
only to the data of the IF- MIB::ifTable and only when public community data queries are
received from IP address 172.16.16.1.

On the other side, if you define the view as:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server view alfaCentauri .1.3.6.1 included
      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server view alfaCentauri

                                                                                                             IF-MIB::ifTable excluded

you will end up with the opposite access restriction for the public community, in the
sense that, from now on, every query received from 172.16.16.1 on behalf of the
community public will be satisfied only if not regarding data included in the
IF- MIB::ifTable.

14.3.3 Configuring SNMPv3 USM-Based
      Authentication

Apart from the above described community-based SNMPv2 cleartext authentication,
the device supports also the more secure SNMPv3 authorization scheme based on
SNMPv3 USM. Currently there is no support for alternate SNMPv3 authorization
schemes like DTLS or SNMP-to-SSH tunneling.

In order to use the native SNMPv3 User Security Model scheme you must define:

    · a valid SNMPv3 user together with its authentication/privacy parameters

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  125/253
Monitoring and diagnostics

         while specifying the group it belongs to. Remember that:
          a user can be part of only one group at a time
          used authorization protocol can be MD5 or SHA1
          used privacy-protocol can be chosen among DES and AES
          authorization/privacy passwords must be 8 characters long minimum

                O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server user testUser group testGroup
                                                                                                  md5 myAuthPass des myPrivPass

    · the access capabilities of the defined groups of users in terms of security-
         level and read/write access views. Remember that the security level associated
         to a group can be:
          noauth: neither authorization nor privacy is enforced for SNMPv3 requests
              coming from a user belonging to such a group.
          auth: every request coming from users belonging to such a group is
              processed using the user's defined authorization protocol and password.
              No privacy encryption is required.
          priv: when an encrypted request is received coming from a user belonging
              to a group having a security-level of priv, after the user is authenticated
              using the chosen authorization-protocol and password, the request is
              decrypted using the user's privacy-protocol and password.

                O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server group-access testGroup
                                                                              auth readview iftable writeview konuendo

           NOTE

           Please note that the view you choose as writeview with the above
           command is a write-only view and not a read-write view.

    · the used views as already explained:

                O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server view iftable
                                                                                                           IF-MIB::ifTable included

                O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server view konuendo
                                                                       KONUENDO-MONITOR-MIB::konEventTable included

In the above example we have defined the new SNMPv3-user testUser as belonging to
SNMPv3-group testGroup, using MD5 as authorization-protocol with password
myAuthPass and DES as privacy-protocol with myPrivPass as privacy-password; then
we have defined the SNMPv3-group testGroup using a security level of auth and
restricting such group access capabilities to view iftable for read request and to view
konuendo for write request. At the end we have taken care to define the needed views
as named in the group-access directive.
Additionally it is possible to manually set the engineID parameter using:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server engineID myCustomEngineID

Just remember that, in case you have not manually set an engineID parameter, the

126/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                              Monitoring and diagnostics

system, during the SNMP AutoDiscovery phase, will use an engineID which is unique
to this device because automatically generated and tied to the MAC address of
management interface.

     IMPORTANT NOTE

     In case you modify the engineID parameter with the SNMP Agent already
     running, the change is not enforced until next Agent restart.

To remove a previously defined user, group-access or engineID simply use these
commands no-form:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure snmp-server engineID
      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure snmp-server user testUser
      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure snmp-server group-access testGroup

14.3.4 Configuring General Server Data

Using the snmp-server configuration sub-commands you can also configure some
common identification parameters regarding the SNMP server instance itself. Such
additional configuration parameters are:

contact      Set the SNMP contact value
description  Set the SNMP server description
location     Set the SNMP location value
name         Set the SNMP server name

These sub-commands may be simply used to help customize your specific machine
SNMP server for identification purposes. Consider that also these SNMP commands,
as the ones above, take effect immediately if the SNMP Agent is running, BUT please
note also that, instead, after issuing a no-form on these sub-commands, in order to
restore their original values, you MUST restart the SNMP subsystem for such an action
to have effect.

Moreover, these commands determines the values exposed by a set of well-known
standard OIDs (RFC1213-MIB::sysName/sysContact/sysLocation/sysDescr), and when
such OIDs are set through these commands they suddenly became read-only from the
SNMP perspective: as long as one of these sub-commands about general server data is
actively modifying the corresponding exposed OID value, such OID will be NO MORE
modifiable through an external SNMP SET operation.

14.3.5 Configuring SNMP Notifications

Beside the classical query/response paradigm, an SNMP Agent is also able to send
asynchronous notification toward a specified destination, which typically runs some
sort of Network Management Entity used to collect data remotely.

These actions are what SNMP calls traps or informs: many kinds of notifications can be
sent by an SNMP Agent on various types of events (both standard and proprietary),
but regardless of the nature of such events a minimum set of system-wide
notification-related parameters must be configured.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4         127/253
Monitoring and diagnostics

This is the purpose of the snmp-server sub-command host and related options, as
briefly showed in the following example:

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server host                  (? pressed)

<ipaddr>  Set the SNMP server target host address for egressing traps

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server host 172.16.16.1      (? pressed)

<community/user> Set the SNMP server trap commmunity or user to be used for

          egressing notifications toward the specified host address

<CR>

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server host 172.16.16.1 trap-comm (? pressed)

v1        Set SNMP protocol Version 1 for egressing traps

v2c       Set SNMP protocol Version 2c for egressing notifications

v3        Set SNMP protocol Version 3 for egressing notifications

<CR>

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server host 172.16.16.1 trap-comm v2c  (?)

informs   Set the SNMP server egressing notifications type to inform

traps     Set the SNMP server egressing-notifications type to trap

      ...

See CLI commands reference (page 217) for more information.

Briefly, the most important and mandatory required data are:

   <ipaddr>: which defines the IP address of the host that will be the target of the
      generated outgoing traps; if this is not set, no trap will be ever generated.

    <community-user>: a community or an SNMPv3 user on behalf of which traps
      are sent. This could be used by target host network management entities to
      classify or discard the received traps themselves.

If no other parameter is specified, the system will emit an SNMPv2 trap toward the
specified host/community pair.

Additionally you can choose to specify also:

 <port>: the destination port to use in case the target host has been configured
   to use a non-standard port for trap reception.

 traps/informs: this token specifies the kind of trap to be sent: an old style
   non-Acknowledged SNMPv2c/v3 Trap or a new SNMP v2c/v3 Acknowledged
   Inform.

 v1/v2c/v3: the SNMP version to use for outgoing notifications. Depending on
   this choice, when you select SNMPv3 notification type, you will be additionally
   presented with the following v3-related notification commands:

    auth/noauth/priv: the desidered minimum security level for outgoing
         SNMPv3 notifications.

    md5 <authpass>/sha1 <authpass>: the desidered authentication
         algorithm and chosen password (minimum 8 characters).

    aes <privpass>/des <privpass>: the desidered encryption algorithm and
         chosen password (minimum 8 characters); this choice is available only if a
         minimum security-level of priv was selected.

128/253                                       Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                          Monitoring and diagnostics

       remoteEngineID <engineID-String>: the remote NMS engineID to be used
            for outgoing SNMPv3 traps (which cannot otherwise complete the SNMPv3
            AutoDiscovery phase).

As soon as the above minimal configuration for outgoing traps is set, the system
begins to emit the following traps without the need of further configuration:

    SNMP Agent coldStart (SNMPv2-MIB::coldStart): each time the agent is started or
      restarted (or after a successful boot).

    SNMP Agent nsNotifyShutdown (NET-SNMP-AGENT-MIB::nsNotifyShutdown):
      each time the agent is stopped or the system is rebooted.

    SNMP Agent nsNotifyRestart (NET-SNMP-AGENT-MIB::nsNotifyRestart): each time
      the configuration is changed while the agent is running it reloads the
      configuration.

    SNMP Agent authenticationFailure (SNMPv2-MIB::authenticationFailure): each
      time a v2c/v3 authorization fails and is rejected.

14.3.6 Konuendo Monitoring Facility

The system has an embedded and flexible monitoring engine which can be used to
satisfy a wide-range of notification needs; one of this needs is surely the configurable
emission of diagnostic traps.

Once a notification trap is enabled for some sort of event by the means of proper
commands, a corresponding event is handled to the engine for monitoring and a full
description of the registered entity is reported in the special SNMP table
KONUENDO- MONITOR- MIB::konEventTable exported by the switch SNMP subsystem;
as soon as such event fires, the SNMP subsystem takes care to intercept such inner
engine-notification and packs an SNMP trap containing all the needed details while
updating, when needed, the konEventTable.

This means that an updated list of all currently monitored events is always available for
consultation via SNMP by a Network Management System (when SNMP agent is
properly configured); the provided proprietary MIBs, KONUENDO- MIB and
KONUENDO- MONITOR- MIB, contain a formal and comprehensive description of such
table and the definition of the generic container-trap that is emitted upon a monitored
fired event. In the following you will find a detailed explanation of the commands
enabling the emission of specific traps (once the SNMP subsystem has been
configured in a general way as stated in the previous paragraphs).

Moreover, the payload of such proprietary traps defined in KONUENDO- MONITOR- MIB
is typically composed of:

    konEntityName: carrying the monitored entity internal reference as it appears also
      in konEventTable.konEntity.

    konEntityValue: carrying the value of the monitored konEntityName which
      triggered the condition and the subsequent outgoing notification.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  129/253
Monitoring and diagnostics

14.3.7 Enabling SNMP Notifications

All the available SNMP notifications can be activated from the global configuration-
level:

O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server  (? pressed)

...              Configure emission of selected traps on all available
   traps-all-if  interfaces at one time
                 Configure emission of global generic traps.
   traps-global  Configure emission of ttcmp related traps
   traps-ttcmp

and are grouped in families:

 traps-global - these traps are generic for the system and not tied to a specific
   interface:

 overtemp (KONUENDO-MONITOR-MIB::konOverTemperature /
      konNormalTemperature): emitted when system operating temperature is
      above normal operating level.

 high-cpu-load (KONUENDO-MONITOR-MIB::konHighCpuLoad /
      konNormalCpuLoad): emitted when system CPU computational level is
      above normal operating threshold for a sustained period of time (5 minutes).

 configuration-change (KONUENDO-MONITOR-MIB::konConfigChange): a
      configuration command has been issued on the system.

 sysadmin-change (KONUENDO-MONITOR-MIB::konSysAdmChange): a
      system-administration command has been issued on the system.

    new-root (BRIDGE-MIB::newRoot): the bridge controlled by the sending
         agent has been elected as the new rootBridge of the calculated spanning-
         tree.

    topology-change (BRIDGE-MIB::topologyChange): emitted when any NON
         EDGE port on the bridge controlled by the sending agent has transitioned to
         the Forwarding State.

 traps-all-if - these, instead, are per-interface traps (such as linkUp/linkDown) and
   from here you can activate such traps on all applicable interfaces at a time:

    link-status (IF-MIB::linkDown/linkUp): emitted when one of the links goes
         Up or Down. In this case a standard IF- MIB::LinkUp IF- MIB::LinkDown trap is
         emitted.

    link-flap (KONUENDO-MONITOR-MIB::konLinkFlapping): emitted when
         one link goes repeatedly Up/Down for more than 5 times within the last 10
         seconds.

 link-poe (KONUENDO-MONITOR-MIB::konPoeUp/konPoeDown): emitted
      when a PoE capable port starts/stops powering an attached device (please
      note that when a PoE-capable port's administrative status is set to 'disabled',
      the related PoE functionality is NOT concurrently turned off, so no trap is
      supposed to be emitted in this case).

130/253                                  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                        Monitoring and diagnostics

    traps-ttcmp - these are traps related to the T TCMP subsystem:

       ttcmp-anomaly (KONUENDO-MONITOR-
            MIB::konTtcmpAnomalyDetected/konTtcmpAnomalyCleared): emitted
            when T TCMP subsystem (if active) detects an anomaly or when an anomaly
            is cleared.

In case you wish to activate all the traps of a family at a time you simply use the
keywork ALL when specifying enabled traps for that family:

      O@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure snmp-server traps-global ALL

and you will end up with all of the available traps for that family enabled. Please note
that the command parsing is case sensitive and the ALL parameter must be all
uppercase (all will not work).

14.4 LLDP

Link Layer Discovery Protocol (LLDP) is a standard protocol used by network devices
for advertising their identity and capabilities on an Ethernet network.

Each LLDP enabled device transmits LLDP packets out from all ports on which the
protocol is enabled. LLDP devices that receive these packets create and maintain a list
of LLDP neighbours they are receiving information from.

If no LLDP packet is received from a known neighbour for a defined amount of time
the device is automatically removed from the list.

LLDP packets are sent to a special multicast address that 802.1D compliant bridges do
not forward. In this case neighbouring can be formed only between directly connected
devices.

LLDP protocol is enabled by default on all Consist Switch models.

You can get a list of the current known neighbours using the command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show lldp neighbours

The result is a list of neighbour devices, not only switches, like this:

Local port  ChassisID          SysName  Capabilities
----------  -----------------
e1-7        00:1d:b3:52:46:80  -------  ------------
e1-7        a0:59:3a:03:00:ca
                               HP ProCurve Sw bridge/switch

                               SW-Std.04-01-CB TTCMP bridge/switch

If you need more detailed information about neighbors you can use the detail option
of the show command. It this case the result, for each neighbor, is like this:

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                                     131/253
Monitoring and diagnostics

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show lldp neighbours detail
      LLDP neighbours discovered
      ===========================
      ChassisId: a0:59:3a:03:00:ca
      System name: SW-Std.03-00-CA
      Description: VDS Consist switch
      Management addr: 10.200.100.1
      Capabilities: TTCMP bridge/switch
      Port id: e2-0 (if_name)
      Port description:
      TTL: 15 secs
      Local interface: e1-7
      TTL remaining: 11

14.4.1 Configuring LLDP options

You can globally disable the protocol from global configuration level using the
command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure lldp run

or just disable it on a specific interface. For example:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure interface e0-1 lldp enable

You can also set the frequency at which LLDP packets are transmitted:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure lldp timer 10

or define the number of seconds that neighbors should wait before removing this
device from their neighbors list if no packet is received:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure lldp holdtime 120

You can get information about current LLDP settings using the show lldp command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show lldp
      Global LLDP information:

          LLDP protocol is running
          LLDP holdtime advertised 15 secs
          LLDP packets sent every 5 secs
          TTCMP extensions are enabled

132/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                       Multicast

Chapter 15 - Multicast

This chapter describes Consist Switch multicast IP traffic capabilities and
configuration.
Multicast packets are packets sent by one host to multiple hosts. On train networks
there are many applications that use multicast packets to transmit information using
this one-to-many relationship. Video surveillance, multimedia devices and passenger
information systems are common applications that frequently use IP multicast
streams. Furthermore several industrial protocols used by TCMS applications use a
publisher/subscriber communication model based on multicast packets.

15.1 Multicast filtering

By default multicast packets are treated by switches as broadcast packets and so
flooded out from all their ports.
With an increase of the number of multicast applications, this behavior could easily
overload the whole network. In this case multicast filtering can be used to prune
multicast traffic so that it travels only to those end destinations that require the traffic,
reducing the amount of useless traffic on the network.
Depending on the complexity of the network, the number of multicast addresses to
handle and the L3 protocol used, multicast forwarding rules can be statically
configured or dynamically build by "snooping" some high level multicast protocol.

15.2 Static filtering

By default multicast filtering is disabled and multicast packets are flooded out from all
switch ports as if they were broadcast packets.
You can change this behavior by disabling flooding of unknown multicast packets
from all ports with the global command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>no configure ip igmp snooping flood-unknown

In this way multicast filtering is enabled and multicast packets can be forwarded only
to those ports that are connected with the multicast end-stations.
Alternatively you can operate on a per port basis enabling or disabling multicast
filtering on a single ports, for example issuing the following interface command:

      A@SW-std.03-00-CA>no configure interface e0-1 ip igmp-snooping flood-unknown

With multicast filtering active you must manually define the ports where multicast
packets should be forwarded to.
You can do this adding one or more static entries to the layer 2 forwarding table or,

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  133/253
Multicast

working at IP layer, associating an ip multicast address with a specific port.
In the first case you can use the global command:

      configure mac-address <l2-mcast-addr> interface <if-name> vlan <vid>

In the second case you can use the interface command:

      config interface <if> ip igmp-snooping static-group <mcast-group> [vlan <vid>]

In this case the associated vlan can be defined only on trunk ports and not on access
ports.

As an example we could make some examples based on the following topology:

           R       R

                                                                S
Here we have a single multicast source (S) with two multicast receivers (R). You could
configure switches as follow:

    · globally disable unknown multicast flooding on all switches

    · enable unknown multicast flooding on ports where the receivers are
         connected as well on all "backbone" ports.

In this case the static configuration is quite simple but, as soon as you add a second
multicast source (S2) and a couple of receivers things become immediately more
complex.

           R1  R2  R1

                              R2

                                                         S2 S1
In fact, in this case, beside disabling unknown multicast flooding on all switches you
should explicitly configure the forwarding rules for both streams on all backbone and
edge ports connected with receivers.

15.3 IGMP snooping

Today most of the network traffic is based on IPv4 protocol. The Internet Engineering
Task Force (IETF) has defined IGMP (Internet Group Management Protocol) a protocol
used by hosts and multicast routers to establish multicast group memberships.
When enabled on a switch, IGMP snooping listens for join/leave multicast group

134/253            Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                                 Multicast

messages, transmitted by end devices, and dynamically updates the Layer 2
forwarding table.

In this way multicast packets can be forwarded only to ports connected with devices
that have registered their interest on a specific multicast stream avoiding unnecessary
flooding to the other ports.

The Consist Switch supports IGMP version 1, 2 and 3.

This registration must be periodically renewed by the end devices otherwise, after a
configurable amount of time, it will be automatically aged out.

For this reason, periodical membership queries should be transmitted by a multicast
router or by a switch acting as a querier and forwarded to all ports by the other
switches; the igmp responses coming from the end devices are used to keep
membership information up to date.

15.3.1 Configuring IGMP snooping

By default IGMP snooping is disabled and multicast traffic is flooded out from all
forwarding ports.

IGMP snooping can be enabled by issuing the global command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure ip igmp snooping enable

When IGMP snooping is enabled, join/leave requests are used by the switch to
forward multicast traffic only to interfaces that are connected to active interested
receivers.

Each host that wants to receive a multicast stream must sent a JOIN request for that
specific group address; when it wants to stop receiving the stream it must send a
LEAVE request. The switch make use of these requests for updating its forwarding
rules.

Incoming multicast packets that are not associated with any switch port (unknown),
depending on the flood- unknown global or per port setup, could be forwarded to
some ports or are silently dropped.

As already mentioned, the active multicast forwarding table must be periodically
refreshed; the aging time for registered groups is set by default to 260 seconds but can
be changed to a value between 100 and 1000 seconds using the following command:

      configure ip igmp snooping aging-time <100-1000>

You can view the current status of the IGMP snooping subsystem issuing the
command:

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  135/253
Multicast

@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show ip igmp

IGMP snooping is enabled
   -Robustness: 2 sec
   -Aging time: 100 sec
   -Immediate leave: disabled
   -TCN ignore: disabled
   -TCN flood query count: 2
   -TCN query solicitation: disabled

Unknown multicast flooding is enabled
Flood to cpu is disabled

IGMP querier is disabled
   -Querier IGMP version: 2
   -Query interval: 125 sec
   -Querier timeout: 70 sec
   -Max response time 10 sec

Active IGMP queriers:

         Address       Vlan Type  Port

         ---------------------------------------

         192.168.4.31         1 igmp v2 e0-1

Active mrports:

           Name Type
           -----------------
           e0-1 dynamic

Registered groups:

           Group address Port Vlan Type           Expires Last reporter

-------------------------------------------------------------------

           239.10.10.1 e0-0    1 igmp v2          01:24 192.168.4.111

239.255.255.250 e0-0           1 igmp v2          01:28 192.168.4.211

           224.0.0.251 e0-0    1 igmp v2          01:25 169.254.136.108

The active configuration parameters, the active queriers and the list of registered
multicast addresses together with their associated ports and VLAN ID are reported on
the console.

15.3.2 IGMP immediate leave processing

When an IGMP version 2 leave message is received on a port, a timer (leave timer) is
started. If no membership report for the specific multicast address is received on the
port for the duration of the leave timer, the multicast address is removed from the
port, otherwise the timer is stopped and the multicast registration remains active.

This timer is necessary to allow other receivers that might be connected to the same
ports to respond to the specific IGMP query transmitted by the multicast router upon
reception of the leave message.

By enabling IGMP immediate leave processing, the switch immediately stop
forwarding a multicast stream to a port as soon as an IGMP version 2 leave message is
received from that port.

IGMP immediate leave can be enabled by issuing the command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure ip igmp snooping immediate-leave

You should not use the immediate leave processing feature where multiple hosts are

136/253                                           Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                                 Multicast

connected to a single port because the leave process could affect other hosts
registered for receiving for the same group.

15.4 Configuring IGMP querier

Multicast membership must be periodically renewed by the end devices otherwise, as
soon as the associated aging timer expires, the multicast address is removed from the
port and the flow is no more forwarded to the end device.
Membership renewal is normally triggered by IGMP queries transmitted by a multicast
router. If there is no multicast router available on the network the switch itself can be
configured to operate as multicast querier issuing the command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure ip igmp querier enable

VLAN1 management interface must be properly configured with an IP address
otherwise no QUERY message is transmitted.
Only one querier should be active at a time. If the querier service is enabled on more
than one switch, an election is automatically performed and the switch with the lower
ip address assigned to the management interface becomes the active querier for the
LAN while the querier activity on the other switches is suspended.
Note that the interval between query messages must be less than the aging timer of
join messages. The default value of query interval is 125 seconds while the default value
of aging time is 260 seconds. You can change these values using the commands:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure ip igmp snooping aging-time 120
      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure ip igmp querier query-interval 60

which, in this example, set the aging time and the query interval at 120 and 60 seconds
respectively.

15.5 Using multiple devices

Things get much more complicated when multiple switches or other devices are used.
The IGMP forwarding rules and the possible issues that may occur with redundant
topologies must be understood for operate correctly.

15.5.1 IGMP forwarding

To determine how to forward multicast traffic the IGMP snooping subsystem need to
known how the active multicast router/querier can be reached. In fact IGMP standard
defines that unknown multicast flows as well as IGMP join and leave messages should
be delivered to the multicast router for further processing.
This is why switch ports where IGMP query messages are received are automatically
defined as mrouter (multicast router) interfaces.
You can also statically configure an interface to be a multicast router interface. A

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  137/253
Multicast

statically configured interface is not subject to aging and does not require an IGMP
querier for IGMP snooping to learn about the interface. You can have a mix of statically
configured and dynamically learned interfaces on a switch by issuing the following
interface command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e0-1 ip igmp-snooping mrouter

15.5.2 Handling STP topology changes

If you are using a redundant topology, special care should be given on path changes
introduced by the spanning tree process, otherwise multicast streams path update will
rely only on general queries transmitted by the active querier and so the process could
require up to query interval seconds.
By default, whenever a topology change notification (TCN) is noticed, the IGMP
process starts flooding multicast streams out from all ports until a predefined number
of General Query is received.
By default the number of general queries for which multicast traffic is flooded is two
but it can be changed using the command:

      configure ip igmp snooping tcn-flood-query-count <1-10>

At the same time of multicast flooding the current spanning-tree root send a special
IGMP leave message, called Query Solicit, with a group address of 0.0.0.0. When the
active querier or the multicast router receives the Query Solicit message, it send out a
General Query which all IGMP listeners respond to. This immediately update the
snooping information throughout the network allowing the multicast data traffic to be
recovered almost instantly
Query solicitation is active by default on the root bridge but can enabled on any
switch, whether or not it is the spanning-tree root, by issuing the following global
command:

      configure ip igmp snooping tcn-query-solicit

To get the network to converge faster, use the command

      configure ip igmp querier max-response-time <1-30>

and set a low response time value, such as one or two seconds, so that the clients will
respond immediately with a report as a response to the IGMP Queries
Reaction to TCN events is enabled by default but can be disabled by issuing the
following global command:

      configure ip igmp snooping tcn-ignore

15.5.3 IGMP filters

Multicast IGMP packets (join/leave) can be be filtered globally or on per port basis.
Filtering IGMP join requests can be useful to limit the flowing of one or more streams

138/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                                                 Multicast

through some parts of the L2 network.
IGMP filters can be configured globally using the global command:

      configure ip igmp snooping filter <maddress> <mask>

or on per interface basis using the interface command:

      configure interface <if-name> ip igmp-snooping filter <maddress> <mask>

On both commands <maddress> is the multicast group whose IGMP related messages
should be ignored and dropped.
The optional <mask> parameter allow the definition of a range of addresses to be
filtered like in the following example:

      configure ip igmp snooping filter 239.10.10.0 255.255.255.0

will filter all the IGMP packets related to the addresses in the range 239.10.10.0 to
239.10.10.255.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  139/253
                                                                            Power over Ethernet (PoE)

Chapter 16 - Power over Ethernet
  (PoE)

16.1 Power Over Ethernet

The 8 ports labeled e1-0 to e1-7 may be optionally equipped with Power over Ethernet
(P o E ).
Each port can detect and power all PoE defined classes (0 to 4), with a maximum power
delivered per port of 15.40 W (class 0, 3 and 4).
For the 14 ports device models this maximum is raised to 30.0 W.
If the electric power for PoE is supplied by an external 48 Vdc power supply, the total
amount of power provided is limited by the power supply maximum capacity.
In the models where the PoE is supplied by an internal power supply, the total amount
of power provided cannot exceed 60 W ca.
When a PoE network device is connected to a port and its power request is compatible
with the system capabilities, it is powered on and the PoE green LED is switched on.
If a PoE device requests more power than it is allowed for its class, the power delivering
is cut off and the PoE LED will turn red. Every 30 seconds ca. the system will try to
power on the device.
If the total amount of the power requested is greater than the power available from
the internal supply the system tries to maintain the power delivery within the limit by
means of a controlled switching off of PoE devices.
The policy used is a fixed priority one, connected with the port number: the lesser the
port number the greater the priority, so, for example, port e1-2 has a greater priority
than port e1-4.
So the system will try to switch off the PoE powered devices starting with port e1-7,
going back to port e1-0. As soon as the total power requested will go back within the
limit, the process will stop.
Every 30 seconds ca. devices will be tentatively switched back on repeating the
process from the beginning.
All critical events are logged.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  141/253
Power over Ethernet (PoE)

It is possible to verify the PoE status with the command:

A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>show poe

Port Mode Status Class              Power (W)
------------------------------------------
e1-0 auto off               -       0.00
e1-1 auto on               class 3  15.67
e1-2 auto off               -       0.00
e1-3 auto on               class 1  3.96
e1-4 auto off               -       0.00
e1-5 auto off               -       0.00
e1-6 auto off               -       0.00
e1-7 auto on               class 2  2.32

16.2 PoE mode

The PoE subsystem behavior, for each PoE port, may be modified in the following way:
    · mode on: the power supply is always active, not depending on the port link
         status (default).
    · mode off: the power supply is always disabled
    · mode as-link: the power supply follows the port link status, so it is active
         when the link is up, it is disabled when the port link is down.

The PoE port mode may be modified with the command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>configure interface e1-0 poe mode on

where the mode qualifier may be on, off or as-link.

17.2 Importing, exporting and deleting
    system configuration

System configuration can be exported to an external network resource using the
command sequence:

      save startup-config
      sysadmin save configfile URL

where URL must be a valid URL with ftp or scp network scheme, pointing to a directory
(not a file) of a reachable network resource.
This action will upload a file in Konuendo Archive Data (KAD) format to the given
network resource. This file can be used to restore the configuration or to deploy it to
other devices by means of a couple of mechanisms. One can copy the KAD file to a
network service, as explained in Managing device firmware, and use the command:

      sysadmin load URL

where URL contains the reference to the file just copied. The K AD file has notion of its
own contents, so the load operation is able to tell, just looking inside the KAD file, if
the file is a configuration dump file or an OS image one and behave accordingly.
These load operations do not modify the switch behavior. To make the action effective,
a specific activation command must be issued:

    · for O.S. Image loading:

                sysadmin set default image x.y.z

    · for configuration loading:

                save startup-config

     NOTE

     The device must be rebooted for these operations to take effect.

Otherwise, the KAD file may be copied on a USB key prepared for system
maintenance (see Konuendo Total Device Management section for details)
You can also save the configuration file in plain text format by appending the plain
keyword to the above command:

      sysadmin save configfile URL plain

The startup configuration file can be deleted from the non volatile memory using the

144/253                               Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                        System administration

command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-CA>sysadmin delete startup-config

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                   145/253
                                       REST Interface

Chapter 18 - REST Interface

18.1 VDS Rail Software Platform REST
    Interface

Every VDS Rail device belonging to the Konuendo product line is equipped with a REST
programmatic interface.

REpresentational State Transfer (REST) is a style of software architecture for distributed
hypermedia systems such as the World Wide Web.

The subset included in the VDS Rail products permits the user interaction and the
device configuration.

Each request, in form of a well defined URI, gets an XML answer through the HT TP
protocol.

The XML nature of the results is a guarantee of easy parsing and understanding of the
returned data.

The queries may be HT TP or HT TPS based.

The queries may be embedded in the user applications by means of well known web
interaction libraries.

The request structure is:

      http://device_ip_address:9901/exec_command?query=configuration_command

or, in case of HT TPS:

      https://device_ip_address:9443/exec_command?query=configuration_command

where:

    · device_ip_address is the IP address of the device to query and
    · configuration_command is the command to send with the blanks substituted

         with the char '+'.
For example: "show version" will be written as show+version.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  147/253
REST Interface

18.2 Examples

Here are some query examples performed on network devices from a web browser in
order to have a quick look at the XML formatted results:

      Q: http://10.200.2.100:9901/exec_command?query=show+spanning-tree
      A:
      <answer status="OK">

          <query>show spanning-tree</query>

                     <timers HT="2" MA="20" FD="15">
                         Hello Time 2 sec Max Age 20 sec Forward Delay 15 sec
                     </timers>
                 </root_bridge>
                 <bridge>
                     <bridge_id prio="32768" mac-addr="a0:59:3a:80:08:f0">
                         32768/a0:59:3a:80:08:f0
                     </bridge_id>
                     <timers HT="1" MA="10" FD="15">
                         Hello Time 1 sec Max Age 10 sec Forward Delay 15 sec
                     </timers>
                 </bridge>
                 <bridge_ports>
                     <port>
                         <name>e2-0</name>
                         <port_id>128.13</port_id>

              </bridge_instance>

      </answer>

150/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                       TTCMP®

                                       Chapter 19 - TTCMP®

The Train Topology and Configuration Management Protocol (TTCMP®) is a Layer 2
protocol that has been developed to give an answer to some important requirements
of the modern Ethernet networks designed (or to be designed) for most of the trains.

The main aim of this protocol is to simplify the installation and the maintenance of
active and passive network devices (switches, gateways and end devices in general)
made by VDS Rail.

Basically, the user should be able to design the network layout using an off-line tool
that runs on a standard workstation. This activity will produce a description (TTCMP
project) of the whole network with details of network devices, their interconnections,
their names and network addresses, the kind of end devices connected, their network
address and the ports used, etc...

After the network has been physically built, the devices running the TTCMP® know the
network topology in terms of nodes and interconnections.

A project can be loaded on one of the active devices by means of the TTCMP® and,
from there, be automatically spread to all the others active devices. At this point each
device is able to identify itself on the project (matching) and extract and apply its
configuration details.

After this step, the TTCMP® goes on with its monitoring activities to assure that the
current network topology is actually reflecting the TTCMP project design.

Project deployment, discovery and monitoring of the physical network topology are
the main TTCMP® activities.

The advantages provided by this protocol can be summarized as:

     · no need to manually configure each TTCMP® capable device, the configuration
         is extracted from the project file;

     · real time check of the network topology, the active topology is compared with
         the topology described in the project and all the discrepancies are signaled;

     · easy replacement of faulty devices, any faulty device can be replaced with
         brand new (not configured) ones and the TTCMP project file is then
         automatically received and applied by the new one;

     · easy upgrade of the network project, a new release of the project can be
         loaded on one of the devices and immediately diffused to all the other active
         devices.

19.1 Operating principles

The TTCMP® is able to be executed at layer 2 of the ISO/OSI stack, without the need of
any layer 3 addressing (it can be executed on not configured devices). Moreover it runs

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  151/253
TTCMP®

only on Ethernet physical layer.
The first level of discovery is based on the standard Link Layer Discovery Protocol
(LLDP). More information on section 14.4 at page 131. By means of this protocol, each
device discovers other LLDP devices that are directly connected to its ports and
collects some information about them (name, description, management IP address
and other).
The second level of discovery is performed spreading out all these information (and a
lot of others), in a concise form, to all the other TTCMP® enabled devices of the
network. The protocol is based on a series of messages that every TTCMP® capable
device may broadcast and receive.

19.1.1 Terms and definitions

For the purposes of this chapter, the following terms and definitions apply:
    · Anomaly
         Inconsistency between the discovered topology and the one defined by the
         TTCMP project.
    · Confirmation
         The action of permanently store ("freeze") the configuration of a TTCMP
         enabled device on its permanent storage.
    · Consist
         Train set, rake of coaches, single vehicle or a group of vehicles which are not
         separated during ordinary train operation. For multi-coach operative mode,
         each coach is also a consist.
    · Consist network
         Communication network interconnecting communication devices in one
         consist.
    · End Device (ED)
         Unit connected to a Consist Network by means of a switch.
    · Fault
         Error caused by some device failure.
    · Matching
         Action performed by every TTCMP enabled device in order to identify itself
         inside the TTCMP project.
    · TTCMP project
         Description of all the consist network details.
    · Topology
         Cable interconnection and number of devices in the Consist network.

152/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                       TTCMP®

19.1.2 Project loading

The TTCMP® constantly monitors the network topology, even without any active
project, registering device, link and end devices activity, appearance or disappearance.
A TTCMP project reflecting the actual consist network design must be supplied in
order to start the active phases of the protocol.

A project may be loaded by means of an USB-pen drive, as detailed on the chapter 29
(AutoUSB subsystem) on page 211, with the project_diffusion or
parametric_project_diffusion actions, or using a TTCMP library based tool like
KonfStation or Kobserver (ask VDS Rail representative for details about these tools
and the TTCMP library). Once the project has been loaded, it becomes the active one4
and it is spread out to every TTCMP® enabled device.

The next phase is the matching: during this phase every device tries to identify itself on
the project and, in case of success, it extracts and applies the proper configuration.

19.1.3 Confirmation

When all the switches are properly identified and configured, the network can be put
in a different state called confirmed; switching to this state every switch stops trying
to identify itself from the current topology and saves on flash its configuration details
ready to be used at every next boot. After the network has entered the confirmed
state, all the devices are fully configured and operative.

The confirmed state, that "freezes" the current configuration, is reached only if the
device receives a specific TTCMP message or if the designated sysadmin command is
executed on its shell (the details are described in the next sections).

19.2 Configuration

The use of TTCMP® is not mandatory; it does not affect the normal behavior of the
network even if left enabled but not used. It runs on top of LLDP and both are enabled
by default.

In order to to turn off the TTCMP® issue:

      A@SW-Std.81-85-20>no configure lldp ttcmp extensions enable

and verify the status of the subsystem with:

      A@SW-Std.81-85-20>show lldp
      Global LLDP information:

          LLDP protocol is running
          LLDP holdtime advertised 15 secs
          LLDP packets sent every 5 secs
          TTCMP extensions are disabled
      A@SW-Std.81-85-20>show ttcmp

4 Each device can store many projects at the same time, but only one of them can be the
    active project.

19.2.1 Subsystem status

The status of the TTCMP® subsystem can be inspected by means of the following
command:

      A@SWITCH-3-B>show ttcmp

154/253                                  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                            TTCMP®

Global TTCMP information:
   TTCMP protocol version 2 extensions are enabled
   TTCMP subsystem status is: idle
   TTCMP packets signature is: disabled
   TTCMP matching mode is: default-fast
   Consist identifier: undefined
   Coach identifier: 3 (Coach Type 3 1st-2nd class slave coach without WC)
   Composition Head flag: not set

Active project: 2002 Rev. 24 Serial F84CA7F1
Active context: 0
Multi-coach project: Yes

Nodes discovered: 10/2
Nodes matched on project: 10/2
Nodes with anomalies: 10

Topology status is: confirmed

Local TTCMP information:
   Matched on project: Yes
   Anomalies detected: Yes

Device replacement:

Automatic project confirmation: enabled

Automatic cst-id election:             enabled

and this is the detail of each field:

· TTCMP protocol version reports the TTCMP® version the device firmware is
    using and whether or not the TTCMP® subsystem is enabled and running.

· TTCMP subsystem status reports what the TTCMP® subsystem is currently
    doing; possible value are:

 transmitting project data when sending project data on the network;

 receiving project data once the first project data packet is received;

 new project data available once the last project data packet is
     received;

 resetting active project when the reset action of the active project is
     in progress;

 ignore ttcmp announces (this status is actually never used);

 confirming topology when the confirmation action has been started
     from the local device;

 resetting configuration when the reset action of the active project is in
     progress due to an error;

     idle (none of the above).

· TTCMP packets signature reports whether or not sent TTCMP packets are
    signed and received ones are verified.

· TTCMP matching mode reports the matching algorithm in use:

 default is the algorithm that tries to understand which device of the
     project is the local one selecting the one that better fits the actual
     connectivity status;

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4                                       155/253
TTCMP®

          default-fast is the one used for manually forcing a match (it works if the
              hostname of the device is set in advance before switching to this
              algorithm);

          diffusion is the one used in the deployment of the multi-caoch projects;

          no-end-devices is like the default one but it does not take into account
              the end devices;

          none when no algorithm is selected.

    · Consist identifier is the Consist UUID announced by the device; the value
         undefined means that the factory UUID of the device is used.

    · Coach identifier reports the Coach identification number (if set) and the
         optional description of the coach.

    · Composition Head flag reports whether of not the Composition Head flag is
         set in the local device (see Coupling solutions section for further information).

    · Active project reports the name, revision and serial number of the active
         TTCMP project (if any).

    · Active context is the identification number of the project context in use (this
         feature is a legacy of the TTCMP® version 1 and it is actually unused on current
         firmwares).

    · Multi-coach project Yes if the project defines multiple coaches; No otherwise.

    · Nodes discovered reports the number of nodes discovered in the topology
         against the total number of TTCMP devices provided by the project for the
         consist (or coach, if in multi-coach mode).

    · Nodes matched on project reports the number of nodes announcing the
         status of matched against the total number of TTCMP devices provided by the
         project for the consist (or coach, if in multi-coach mode).

    · Nodes with anomalies reports the number of nodes announcing an error or
         anomaly.

    · Topology status confirmed if the configuration has been "frozen";
         unconfirmed otherwise.

    · Matched on project Yes if the device found a full match on the active project,
         P1 if the device found only a partial match, No otherwise; the optional string
         delayed configuration is printed when the device found a match very early
         but the configuration is not applied until the matched status is not announced
         by a significant number of other devices.

    · Anomalies detected Yes in case of faults and/or anomalies; No otherwise.

    · Automatic project confirmation enabled (by default) if the device can turn to
         the confirmed status without receiving a specific command; this feature is used
         when the replacement of a faulty device took place on a confirmed network
         (the brand new device automatically turn to confirmed if it finds a match on
         the project and all the other devices are already confirmed).

156/253  Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4
                                                                            TTCMP®

· Automatic cst-id election enabled (by default) if the device can change its
    Consist UUID making its own the one with lower value received from other
    devices belonging to the same consist; see Coupling solutions section for
    further information about this feature.

19.2.2 Topology

The whole network topology, as seen by means of the TTCMP® actions, can be
examined with the following command:

A@SWITCH-3-B>show topology

SysName     Chassis-id                 Coach-id Port Match Err Project      C

---------------- ------------------ -------- ----- ----- --- --------------- -

SWITCH-1-A  a0:59:3a:80:c2:20 1        e0-0 Yes No 2002/24                  Y

SWITCH-1-A  a0:59:3a:80:c2:50 1        e0-1 Yes No 2002/24                  Y

SWITCH-1-B  a0:59:3a:81:7a:70 1        ist0 Yes No 2002/24                  Y#

SWITCH-1-B  a0:59:3a:80:31:60 1        ist0 Yes No 2002/24                  Y

SWITCH-2-A  a0:59:3a:81:7a:90 2        ist0 Yes Yes 2002/24                 Y

SWITCH-2-B  a0:59:3a:80:27:40 2        e0-0 Yes No 2002/24                  Y

SWITCH-3-A  a0:59:3a:80:c1:c0 3        ist0 Yes No 2002/24                  Y

SWITCH-3-B  a0:59:3a:80:31:30 3        none Yes No 2002/24                  Y#

SWITCH-4-A  a0:59:3a:80:31:50 4        ist0 Yes No 2002/24                  Y

SWITCH-4-B  a0:59:3a:00:01:02 4        e0-1 Yes Yes 2002/24                 Y

This is the meaning of each column:

· SysName is the hostname of the device as seen by the Operating System.

· Chassis-id is the device ID as announced by the TTCMP® subsystem; VDS Rail
    devices use the MAC Address as device ID for TTCMP®.

· Coach-id is the Coach identification number; this field is meaningful only for
    networks where a multi-coach project is in use, otherwise it can be set to 0.

· Port is the name of the interface of the local switch from which the
    announcements sent by the detected device are received.

The last four fields are meaningful only if a TTCMP project is active on the network:

· Match shows if the current switch is matched with the active project.

· Err is set to Yes in case of faults and/or anomalies on the device.

· Project is the name of the active project followed by its revision number.

· C is Y when the topology is confirmed, N otherwise.

If present at the end of a topology line, the hash character indicates that there is a
mismatch between the Consist ID and/or confirmation status announced by the device
and the values of the local one. The actual kind of mismatch can be inspected looking
at the topology details:

A@SWITCH-3-B>show topology detail

    values are:

 VDS Konuendo Vehicular Gateway;

 VDS Integrated TBN;

                                       TTCMP®

          bridge/switch;

          generic network device.

     · Management address is the IP address of the device's management interface.

     · Consist-id is the Consist UUID announced by the device (can be unknown).

     · Coach-id is the Coach ID value set on the device or None5.

     · Active project : is the name of the active project followed by its revision
         number.

     · Project matching status Yes if the device found a full match on the active
         project, P1 if the device found only a partial match, No otherwise.

     · Anomalies detected on this device Yes if the matched device found any
         anomaly in regard to the topology described in the project, No otherwise.

     · Topology confirmed on this device Yes if the configuration of the matched
         device has been confirmed, No otherwise.

     · Conflicts detected on this device are the issues detected by TTCMP® and
         announced by the device; at present time the possible values are None or a
         combination of one or more of the following:

          Coach ID: the device detected a conflict between its own Coach ID value
              and the one announced by the other devices belonging to the same
              coach;

          Composition Head: the device detected that the Composition Head flag is
              also set and announced by other coaches;

          Lock CRC: the device detected that its own Composition Lock CRC differs
              from the one announced by the other devices belonging to the same
              coach;

          Lock Position: the device detected that its own Composition Lock
              Position value differs from the one announced by the other devices
              belonging to the same coach;

          Lock Orientation: the device detected that its own Composition Lock
              Orientation differs from the one announced by the other devices
              belonging to the same coach;

          Circular Reference: the device is not able to detect the current Train
              Composition.

         Except for the last one, all these conflicts are meaningful only for networks
         where a multi-coach project is in use.

     · Device seen on local interface is the interface of the local device where the
         remote device announcements are received.

This is the meaning of each column of the table reporting information about the

5 The value not available can be displayed on devices whose firmware does not
support multi-coach projects.

Consist Switch User Manual ver. 2.0.4  159/253
TTCMP®

interfaces of the remote device:
    · Port is the name of the interface.
    · Status is the interface link status (Up or Down).
    · STP is the port status as seen from the Rapid Spanning Tree Protocol:
          fwd when forwarding;
          lrn when learning;
          blk when blocking;
          dis when disabled.
    · Type is the kind of device connected to this port, from a network point of view;
         it can be:
          ttcmp if the connected device is TTCMP® capable;
          lldp if the connected device is LLDP capable;
          mac for any other end device.
    · Dev gives some hint about the connected device; possible values are gw
         (Gateway), sw (Switch), hb (Hub) and ed (end device).
    · Mac-addr is the MAC address of the connected device (if any); it can happen
         that many devices seem to be directly connected to the interface (i.e. using a
         hub or an unmanaged switch); when they are all end devices the value
         ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff is reported.
    · Err is set to Y in case of anomalies; in that case Code specifies the type of error
         with a value that can be:
          1 when a device is detected but it is not present in the project;
          2 when the project provides for a device on the interface but no device has
              been detected;
          4 when the detected device does not match the type provided by the
              project (i.e. an end device is expected but a switch has been detected);
          8 when the connected device is TTCMP capable but it is not the one
              provided by the project; it can happen in three cases:
               the connected device is unconfigured or configured manually (no
                   project);
               the project on the connected device is different from the local one;
               the by-pass of the actual connected device is closed; this can be
                   temporary if the connected device is booting up or permanent when it
                   is switched off due to a fault.

19.2.3 Project handling

The list of TTCMP projects loaded on the device can be shown with the command:

      A@SW-Std.03-00-DC>sysadmin show projects

      test_release:1
      test_release:2 [current]

      Free projects space: 327.3M

The [current] tag, when present, indicates that the project is the one currently active.
As already stated a project may be loaded and spread out to every TTCMP® enabled
device by means of an USB-pen drive or using a TTCMP library based tool.

the project:
